<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def: histogram}
		Given a nonzero vector $\boldsymbol{\xi} \in \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^{n}$, its \emph{histogram} is the probability measure $\rho_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}$ on $[0,1]$ with density
		\begin{align}
			\rho_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x) := \frac{n}{\norm{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_1}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}(\lceil nx \rceil), \qquad x \in (0,1].
		\end{align}
		For a nonzero matrix $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^{m \times n}$, its \emph{histogram} is the probability measure $\pi_{\mathbf{A}}$ on $[0, 1]^2$ with density
		\begin{align}
			\varphi_{\mathbf{A}}(x, y) :=  \frac{mn}{\norm{\mathbf{A}}_{1}} \mathbf{A}_{\lceil mx \rceil, \lceil ny \rceil}, \qquad (x,y) \in (0,1]^2.
		\end{align}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{assumption: bounded_cost}
		The cost functions $\kappa_k$ converge uniformly on $[0,1]^2$ to a continuous function $\kappa_{\infty}$.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}
		We have $m_k, n_k \to \infty$ and $m_k/n_k \to \gamma \in (0, \infty)$. Moreover, there exist probability measures $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_{\infty}}, \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_{\infty}}$ on $[0,1]$ such that $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k} \to \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_{\infty}}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k} \to \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_{\infty}}$ weakly.
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{assumption: L^1_limits_margins}
		There are positive probability densities $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_{\infty}}$, $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_{\infty}}$ on $[0,1]$ and $\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\infty}}$ on $[0,1]^2$ with
		\begin{align}
			\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\rightarrow \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_{\infty}} \ \text{ in } L^{1}[0, 1], \qquad \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}\rightarrow \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_{\infty}} \ \text{ in } L^{1}[0, 1], \qquad \varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{k}}\rightarrow \varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\infty}} \ \text{ in } L^{1}([0, 1]^2).
		\end{align}
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex: continuous_kappa}
		Let $(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k,\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k)$ satisfy Assumption~\ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, and let $\kappa$ be continuous on $[0,1]^2$, normalized so that $\int e^{-\kappa}\,d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}) = 1$. Set $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{k,ij} := e^{-\kappa(i/m_k,\,j/n_k)}\,\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k(j)$. By the uniform continuity of $e^{-\kappa}$ and the weak convergence of $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$, $\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}_1/N_k^2 = N_k^{-2}\sum_{i,j} e^{-\kappa(i/m_k,\, j/n_k)} \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k(j) \to 1$. By \eqref{eq: def_kappa}, $\kappa_k(x,y) = \kappa\big(\lceil m_kx\rceil/m_k, \lceil n_ky\rceil/n_k\big) - \log\big(N_k^2/\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}_1\big)$, so $\kappa_k \to \kappa$ uniformly and Assumption~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} holds with $\kappa_\infty = \kappa$. If, moreover, the margins discretize densities bounded away from zero and infinity, for instance $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k(i) \approx N_k\int_{(i-1)/m_k}^{i/m_k}\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ up to rounding and similarly for $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k$, with $N_k/m_k, N_k/n_k \to \infty$, then Assumption~\ref{assumption: L^1_limits_margins} holds as well. If $\kappa(x,y) = a(x) + b(y)$, then $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k$ has rank one (Corollary~\ref{cor: product_case}). \hfill $\blacktriangle$
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm: conditioned_model_weak_as_limit_no_rate}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}.
		\begin{description}                  
			\item[(i)] The log partition function of the Gibbs measure \eqref{eq: permutation_measure_continuous} converges to the limiting EOT value:
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: log_parition_limit}
				\lim_{k \to \infty} \frac{1}{N_k} \log \frac{\mathcal{Z}_k(\kappa_k)}{N_k!}
				=  -\Gamma_{\infty}.
			\end{align}
			\item[(ii)] The random measure $\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k}$ converges weakly in probability to $\pi_{\infty}$, that is, $\int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} \to \int g \,d\pi_\infty$ in probability for every $g \in C([0,1]^2)$.
		\end{description}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm: conditioned_model_weak_as_limit}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: L^1_limits_margins}, that $m_k + n_k \to \infty$, and that there is $c \in (0,1]$ such that $\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}_1 \ge c\, m_kn_k\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}_\infty$ for all $k$. Fix $\varepsilon > 0$ and $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$, and suppose that $N_k \geq (m_k + n_k)^{1 + \varepsilon}\log N_k$ for all large $k$. Then there is a constant $C>0$, depending only on $c$, $M$ and $\norm{g}_{\infty}$, such that for all sufficiently large $k$, with probability at least $1 - 2\exp \big( -C(m_k + n_k)^{1+\varepsilon} \log N_k\big)$,
		\begin{multline}
			\label{eq: rate_bound}
			\left| \int_{[0, 1]^2} g \, d(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \pi_{\infty}) \right| \le \sqrt{\frac{(m_k+n_k)^{1+\varepsilon}\log N_k}{N_k}} \\
			+ 2\norm{g}_\infty \Big( 40 \log M \big( \norm{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k} - \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}}_{L^1} + \norm{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k} - \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}}_{L^1} \big) + 11 M^5 d_{H}(\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}, \varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{\infty}}) \Big)^{1/2}.
		\end{multline}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm: permuton_partition_det_limit}
		Suppose that $N_k \to \infty$, $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k} \to \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}\to\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ weakly. Let $f_k$ be grid-step functions with respect to the $m_k \times n_k$ grid that converge uniformly to a continuous function $f_\infty$ on $[0,1]^2$. Let $\xi_\infty := d\pi^{f_\infty}/d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$, where $\pi^{f_\infty} := \pi^{f_\infty}_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}}$, and let $\mathcal{S} : L^2(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}) \to L^2(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty})$ be the integral operator
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: def_S_operator}
			(\mathcal{S} h)(x) := \int \big(\xi_\infty(x,y) - 1\big) h(y)\, \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}(dy).
		\end{align}
		Then $\mathcal{S}$ is a Hilbert--Schmidt operator with $\norm{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 = \chi^2\big(\pi^{f_\infty} \,\Vert\, \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}\big) := \int(\xi_\infty - 1)^2\,d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$ and $\norm{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{op}} \le 1 - e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(f_\infty)}$, and
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: permuton_partition_limit_statement}
			\lim_{k \to \infty} \mathcal{D}_k(f_k) = \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S})^{-1/2} = \prod_{i \ge 1} (1 - s_i^2)^{-1/2},
		\end{align}
		where $s_1 \ge s_2 \ge \cdots \ge 0$ are the singular values of $\mathcal{S}$, i.e., the canonical correlations of $\pi^{f_\infty}$, and $\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}$ is the Fredholm determinant.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor: enumeration}
		Under Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence},
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: enumeration_identity}
			\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k,\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k;\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}}{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k,\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k;\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}} = \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I-\mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S})^{-1/2} =: D_\infty,
		\end{align}
		where $\mathcal{S}$ is the operator of Theorem~\ref{thm: permuton_partition_det_limit} with $f_\infty = \kappa_\infty$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def: cell_average}
		For $g \in L^1([0,1]^2)$, the \emph{cell average} of $g$ is the grid-step function
		\begin{align}
			\mathsf{E} g := \sum_{i,j} \mathbf{G}_{ij}\,\1_{A_{ij}}, \qquad \mathbf{G}_{ij} := mn\int_{A_{ij}} g(x,y)\,dx\,dy.
		\end{align}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def: fluctuation_fields}
		For $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$ and $(u,v) \in [0,1]^2$ set
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: def_fluctuation_field}
			\Phi_k(g) := \sqrt{N_k}\int_{[0,1]^2} g\,d(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{N_k}} \sum_{i,j} \mathbf{G}_{ij}\,(\mathbf{X}_{k,ij} - \mathbf{Z}_{k,ij}), \qquad \mathbb{F}_k(u,v) := \Phi_k\big(\1_{[0,u]\times[0,v]}\big),
		\end{align}
		where $\mathbf{G}_{ij} = m_kn_k\int_{A_{ij}} g(x,y)\,dx\,dy$ as in Definition~\ref{def: cell_average}.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def: SB_white_noise}
		The \emph{Schr\"odinger-bridge white noise} is the centered Gaussian process $\Xi = \{\Xi(g)\}_{g \in L^2(\pi_\infty)}$ with covariance
		\begin{align}\label{eq:covariance_mx_Xi}
			\operatorname{Cov}\big(\Xi(g), \Xi(h)\big) = \big\langle (I-P_{\pi_{\infty}})g, (I-P_{\pi_{\infty}})h\big\rangle_{L^2(\pi_\infty)}.
		\end{align}
		The \emph{Schr\"odinger-bridge pillow} is the two-parameter Gaussian process $\mathbb{F}(u,v) := \Xi(\1_{[0,u]\times[0,v]})$, $(u,v) \in [0,1]^2$, the restriction of $\Xi$ to indicators of rectangles anchored at the origin.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm: CLT}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, and suppose that either (i) $g, g_1, \ldots, g_p$ are bounded Borel functions on $[0,1]^2$ that are continuous at $(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$-almost every point, or (ii) $g, g_1, \ldots, g_p \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$ and the families $\{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\}_k$ and $\{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}\}_k$ are uniformly integrable (for instance, uniformly bounded). Then $\Phi_k(g) \Rightarrow \mathcal{N}(0, V(g))$ and, more generally,
		\begin{align}
			\big(\Phi_k(g_1), \ldots, \Phi_k(g_p)\big) \Longrightarrow \big(\Xi(g_1), \ldots, \Xi(g_p)\big),
		\end{align}
		with convergence of all moments. The same holds if each $g_i$ is replaced by bounded Borel functions $g_{i,k}$ with $\norm{g_{i,k} - g_i}_\infty \to 0$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def: Xi_hat}
		A \emph{distributional version} of $\Xi$ is a random element $\hat\Xi$ of $H^{-\infty}$ such that $\langle\hat\Xi, g\rangle = \Xi(g)$ almost surely for every $g \in H^\infty$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm: functional_limits}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}.
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
			\item A distributional version $\hat\Xi$ of $\Xi$ exists and is almost surely unique, and $\Phi_k \Rightarrow \hat\Xi$ in $H^{-\infty}$ with its strong dual topology.
			\item If, in addition, $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ have no atoms, then the Schr\"odinger-bridge pillow $\mathbb{F}$ has a continuous version, still denoted by $\mathbb{F}$, and $\mathbb{F}_k \Rightarrow \mathbb{F}$ in $C([0,1]^2)$ with the uniform topology.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor: KS}
		Under Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, $\sup_B |\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k}(B) - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}(B)| = O_{\mathbb{P}}(N_k^{-1/2})$, where the supremum is over axis-parallel rectangles $B \subset [0,1]^2$. If, in addition, $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ have no atoms, then 
		\begin{align}
			\sqrt{N_k}\sup_{B}\big|\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k}(B) - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}(B)\big| \Longrightarrow \sup_{B}|\mathbb{F}(B)|, \qquad \int_{[0,1]^2}\mathbb{F}_k(u,v)^2\,du\,dv \Longrightarrow \int_{[0,1]^2}\mathbb{F}(u,v)^2\,du\,dv,
		\end{align}
		where $\int\mathbb{F}_k^2 = N_k\int_{[0,1]^2}(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})([0,u]\times[0,v])^2\,du\,dv$ is the Cram\'er--von Mises statistic.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor: product_case}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, and suppose that $\kappa_\infty \in \mathcal{A}$, i.e., $\kappa_\infty(x,y) = a(x) + b(y)$.  Then $\pi_\infty = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$, $D_\infty = 1$, and $V(g)$ is given by 
		\begin{align}
			\int \Big(g(x,y) - \int g(x,y')\,\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}(dy') - \int g(x',y)\,\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}(dx') + \int g \, d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})\Big)^2 \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}(dx)\,\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}(dy).
		\end{align}
		Moreover, $\mathbb{F}$ has the finite-dimensional distributions of $\big(\mathbb{B}(R(u), C(v))\big)_{u,v}$, where $\mathbb{B}$ is the standard Brownian pillow, the continuous centered Gaussian process on $[0,1]^2$ with covariance $(s\wedge s' - ss')(t\wedge t'-tt')$, and $R$ and $C$ are the distribution functions of $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$; if $R$ and $C$ are continuous, the continuous version of $\mathbb{F}$ has the same law.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor: mallows}
		Let $f$, $W$ and $\mathcal{S}$ be as above. Then Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence} hold for the unit margins and the prior means $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_N := (e^{-f(i/N,j/N)})_{i,j\in[N]}$, with $\pi_\infty = \pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}$ and $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_N} = \pi^{f_N}_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}$. Moreover:
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
			\item \emph{(Partition function.)}
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: mallows_partition}
				\lim_{N\to\infty} e^{N\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f_N)}L_N(f) = \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S})^{-1/2}.
			\end{align}
			For $f \in C^1([0,1]^2)$ and Schr\"odinger potentials $(a,b)$ of $\pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}$, defined on $[0,1]$ by \eqref{eq: schrodinger_system},
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: mallows_pal}
				\lim_{N\to\infty} e^{N\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f)}L_N(f) = e^{-\frac12[a(1) - a(0) + b(1) - b(0)]}\,\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S})^{-1/2},
			\end{align}
			and
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: mallows_tripathi}
				\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_N}\prod_{i=1}^N W\big(\tfrac{i}{N},\tfrac{\sigma(i)}{N}\big) = \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S})^{-1/2}.
			\end{align}
			\item \emph{(Linear statistics.)} Let $\sigma_N \sim \mathsf{P}^f_N$. If $\mathbf{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{L}):[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{L}$ is continuous and $\Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g}) := \sum_{i=1}^N \mathbf{g}\big(\tfrac{i}{N},\tfrac{\sigma_N(i)}{N}\big)$, then
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: mallows_CLT_continuous}
				N^{-1/2} \left( \Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g}) - \mathbb{E}\,\Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g}) \right) \Longrightarrow \mathcal{N}\big(\mathbf{0}, V(\mathbf{g})\big) \qquad\text{and}\qquad N^{-1} \operatorname{Cov}\big(\Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g})\big) \longrightarrow V(\mathbf{g}),
			\end{align}
			where $V(\mathbf{g})$ is the $L\times L$ matrix with entries $V(\mathbf{g})_{\ell\ell'}=\big\langle (I-P)g_{\ell}, (I-P)g_{\ell'}\big\rangle_{L^2(\pi_\infty)}$.
			If $f$ and $\mathbf{g}$ are Lipschitz, then
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: mallows_CLT}
				\sqrt{N}\Big(\frac{\Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g})}{N} - \int W \mathbf{g}\,du\Big) \Longrightarrow \mathcal{N}\big(\mathbf{0}, V(\mathbf{g})\big).
			\end{align}
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm: ESD_limit}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, suppose that $\delta \le \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}, \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k} \le \delta^{-1}$ for some $\delta \in (0,1]$ and all $k$, that $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k} \to \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k} \to \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ in $L^1[0,1]$, and that there is $c_0 > 0$ such that $\bar s_k \ge c_0$ for all $k$ and $\log\bar s_k = o(m_k)$. Let $W_\infty := d\pi_\infty/du$ and $\theta := \gamma/(1+\gamma)$. Then there is a unique probability measure $\nu_\infty$ on $[0,\infty)$ whose Stieltjes transform coincides with $\int_0^1 G(x,z)\,dx$ for all $z \in \mathbb{H}$ with $\operatorname{Im}z \ge 2\norm{W_\infty}_\infty + 1$, where $G(\cdot,z)$ is the solution of \eqref{eq: dyson_continuum} with $W = W_\infty$. Moreover,
		\begin{align}
			\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}_k}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}_k}^\top} \Longrightarrow \nu_\infty \qquad\text{and}\qquad \mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}^\top} \Longrightarrow \nu_\infty \qquad \text{weakly in probability}.
		\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem: ESD_permutations}
		By \eqref{eq:coupling_Gibbs}, Theorem~\ref{thm: ESD_limit} is equivalently a statement about Gibbs random permutations. Namely, with the block counts $\mathbf{X}(\sigma)$ of \eqref{eq:construction_Gibbs_X}, the conclusion of Theorem~\ref{thm: ESD_limit} holds for $\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}(\sigma_k)}$ with $\sigma_k \sim \mathsf{P}_k$. Thus, at the level of the global spectrum, the block counts of a Gibbs permutation behave like independent Poisson variables with means $\mathbf{Z}_{k,ij}$, and the variance profile $W_\infty$ is the density of $\pi_\infty = (T_\infty)_{\#}\mu_\infty$, where $\mu_\infty$ is the limiting optimal permuton. If the limiting margins are uniform, then $\nu_\infty$ is the same rescaled Marchenko--Pastur law for every limiting cost (Section~\ref{sec: bg_spectral}). For instance, let $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij} = e^{-f(i/m,j/m)}$ for a continuous function $f$ on $[0,1]^2$ and let all margins be equal to $d$, so that $\sigma$ is a Gibbs permutation of $[md]$ whose kernel is constant on $d \times d$ blocks. If $m \to \infty$, $d/m$ is bounded below and $\log d = o(m)$, then the $m \times m$ matrix of block counts has this Marchenko--Pastur limit for every $f$; see Figure~\ref{fig: perm_ESD}. The theorem does not apply to unit margins, for which $\bar s = 1/N$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: permutation_CT_coupling}
		Let $\sigma \sim \mathsf{P}$ and let $\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{X}(\sigma)$ be its block counts \eqref{eq:construction_Gibbs_X}. Then the following hold.
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
			\item $\mathbf{X} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$ and $\mathbf{X} \sim \lambda_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}; \boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$, that is, \eqref{eq:coupling_Gibbs} holds. Moreover, Barvinok's identity \eqref{eq: barvinok_per_identity} holds.
			\item For every $g \in L^1([0,1]^2)$, $\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^2} g \, d\pi_{\mathbf{X}} = \int_{[0,1]^2} (\mathsf{E} g)\circ T \, d\mu_{\sigma}$.
			\item The measures $\pi_{\mathbf{X}}$ and $T_{\#}\mu_\sigma$ assign the same mass $\mathbf{X}_{ij}/N = \mu_\sigma\big(T^{-1}(A_{ij})\big)$ to each cell $A_{ij}$. Consequently, for every $g \in C([0,1]^2)$ with modulus of continuity $\omega_g$,
			\begin{align}
				\left| \int_{[0, 1]^2} g \, d\pi_{\mathbf{X}} - \int_{[0, 1]^2} g \circ T \, d\mu_{\sigma} \right| \le \omega_g\big(\sqrt{m^{-2} + n^{-2}}\big).
			\end{align}
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem: quantile_properties}
		For all $i \in [m]$ and $j \in [n]$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: preimage_of_cell}
			T^{-1}(A_{ij}) = \Big( \tfrac{\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i-1)}{N}, \tfrac{\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}{N}\Big] \times \Big( \tfrac{\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j-1)}{N}, \tfrac{\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}{N}\Big] = \bigcup_{k,\ell\,:\, Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k) = i,\ Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\ell) = j} B_{k\ell}.
		\end{align}
		In particular, $T(B_{k\ell}) \subset A_{Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k), Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\ell)}$ for all $k, \ell \in [N]$, and for every $\sigma \in S_N$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: permuton_mass_of_cell}
			\mu_{\sigma}\big(T^{-1}(A_{ij})\big) = \frac{1}{N} \,\#\big\{k \in [N] : Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k) = i,\ Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\sigma(k)) = j \big\}.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Since $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}} = m\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)/N$ on $\big(\frac{\tau-1}{m}, \frac{\tau}{m}\big]$, we have $F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(i/m) = \bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)/N$ for all $i$. As $F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}$ is a continuous increasing bijection of $[0,1]$ with inverse $q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}$, it follows that $q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(s) \in \big(\frac{i-1}{m}, \frac{i}{m}\big]$ if and only if $s \in \big(\frac{\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i-1)}{N}, \frac{\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}{N}\big]$. The same holds for $q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}$, which proves the first equality in \eqref{eq: preimage_of_cell}. The second equality holds because $Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k) = i$ if and only if $\bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i-1) < k \le \bar\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)$, by the definition \eqref{eq: index_quantile}. Finally, $\mu_\sigma$ assigns mass $1/N$ to each square $B_{k\sigma(k)}$ and no mass to the squares $B_{k\ell}$ with $\ell \ne \sigma(k)$, which gives \eqref{eq: permuton_mass_of_cell}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
(i) Let $R_i := Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}^{-1}(i)$ and $C_j := Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}^{-1}(j)$, so that $|R_i| = \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)$ and $|C_j| = \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)$. The $i$th row sum of $\mathbf{X}(\sigma)$ is $|R_i|$ and, since $\sigma$ is a bijection, its $j$th column sum is $|C_j|$, so $\mathbf{X}(\sigma) \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$. Since $\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$ equals $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}$ on $R_i \times C_j$, the weight of $\sigma$ in \eqref{eq: gibbs_intro} depends on $\sigma$ only through its block counts:
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: block_weight}
			\prod_{k=1}^{N}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{k\sigma(k)} = \prod_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{X}_{ij}(\sigma)}.
		\end{align}
		
		Fix $\mathbf{x} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$. The permutations $\sigma$ with $\mathbf{X}(\sigma) = \mathbf{x}$ correspond bijectively to the following choices: for each $i$, a partition of $R_i$ into labeled sets $S_{i1}, \ldots, S_{in}$ of sizes $\mathbf{x}_{i1}, \ldots, \mathbf{x}_{in}$ (the elements mapped into $C_1, \ldots, C_n$), for each $j$, a partition of $C_j$ into labeled sets $S'_{1j}, \ldots, S'_{mj}$ of sizes $\mathbf{x}_{1j}, \ldots, \mathbf{x}_{mj}$ (their images), and bijections $S_{ij} \to S'_{ij}$. Therefore
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: permutation_count}
			\#\{\sigma \in S_N : \mathbf{X}(\sigma) = \mathbf{x}\} = \prod_{i=1}^m\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!}{\prod_{j}\mathbf{x}_{ij}!}\prod_{j=1}^n\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}{\prod_{i}\mathbf{x}_{ij}!}\prod_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{ij}! = \frac{\prod_{i}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!\prod_{j}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}{\prod_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{ij}!}.
		\end{align}
		Combining \eqref{eq: block_weight} and \eqref{eq: permutation_count}, for every $\mathbf{x} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: block_count_law}
			\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{X}(\sigma) = \mathbf{x}\big) = \frac{1}{\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}})}\sum_{\sigma' :\, \mathbf{X}(\sigma') = \mathbf{x}}\ \prod_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{x}_{ij}} = \frac{\prod_{i}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!\prod_{j}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}{\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}})}\prod_{i,j}\frac{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{x}_{ij}}}{\mathbf{x}_{ij}!}.
		\end{align}
		Thus $\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{X}(\sigma) = \mathbf{x}) \propto \prod_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{x}_{ij}}/\mathbf{x}_{ij}!$ on $\mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$, which is \eqref{eq: conditioned_model}. Moreover, since the probabilities in \eqref{eq: block_count_law} sum to one over $\mathbf{x} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$, we obtain Barvinok's identity \eqref{eq: barvinok_per_identity}.
		
		(ii) By \eqref{eq: permuton_mass_of_cell} and \eqref{eq: cell_average_invariance},
		\begin{align}
			\int (\mathsf{E} g) \circ T\,d\mu_\sigma = \sum_{i,j} \mathbf{G}_{ij}\,\mu_\sigma\big(T^{-1}(A_{ij})\big) = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{G}_{ij}\mathbf{X}_{ij} = \int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{X}}.
		\end{align}
		
		(iii) By \eqref{eq: permuton_mass_of_cell}, $(T_{\#}\mu_\sigma)(A_{ij}) = \mathbf{X}_{ij}/N = \pi_{\mathbf{X}}(A_{ij})$. If two measures have the same mass $w$ on a set $A$, then the difference of the integrals of $g$ over $A$ is at most $w \cdot \sup_{p, p' \in A}|g(p) - g(p')|$. Summing over the cells, whose diameter is $\sqrt{m^{-2} + n^{-2}}$, proves the first bound.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{thm: transference}
		Fix positive integer margins $(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}) \in \mathbb{N}^{m} \times \mathbb{N}^{n}$ with $\norm{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}_{1} = \norm{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}_1 = N$ and a prior mean $\boldsymbol{\Lambda} \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m \times n}$. Let $\mathbf{X} \sim \lambda_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}; \boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$ and $\mathbf{Y} \sim \operatorname{Poisson}(\mathbf{Z}^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}})$. Then for measurable $h : \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} \to [0,\infty)$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}[h(\mathbf{X})] \leq \frac{(eN)^{m+n}}{m^m n^n} \mathbb{E}[h(\mathbf{Y})].
		\end{align}
		In particular, $\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{X} \in B) \leq (eN)^{m+n} m^{-m} n^{-n}\, \mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Y} \in B)$ for every Borel set $B \subset \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq: same_conditional_law} (see Section~\ref{sec: bg_poisson}), $\mathbf{X}$ has the conditional law of $\mathbf{Y}$ given $\{\mathbf{Y} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})\}$. Since $h \ge 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}[h(\mathbf{X})] = \mathbb{E}\big[h(\mathbf{Y}) \,\big|\, \mathbf{Y} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})\big] \leq \frac{\mathbb{E}[h(\mathbf{Y})]}{\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Y} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}))},
		\end{align}
		so it suffices to show that $\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Y} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})) \geq (eN)^{-(m+n)}m^{m}n^{n}$. Since $\sum_{i,j} \mathbf{Z}_{ij} = N$, the definition of $\mathbf{Y}$ and Barvinok's identity \eqref{eq: barvinok_per_identity} give
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: ind_model_lower_bd_pf_1}
			\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{Y} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})\big) = \sum_{\mathbf{x} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})} \prod_{i, j}e^{-\mathbf{Z}_{ij}} \frac{\mathbf{Z}_{ij}^{\mathbf{x}_{ij}}}{\mathbf{x}_{ij}!} = e^{-N} \sum_{\mathbf{x} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})} \prod_{i,j} \frac{\mathbf{Z}_{ij}^{\mathbf{x}_{ij}}}{\mathbf{x}_{ij}!} = e^{-N}\frac{\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\mathbf{Z}})}{\prod_{i = 1}^{m}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!  \prod_{j = 1}^n \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}.
		\end{align}
		Let $\mathbf{B}_{ij} := \mathbf{Z}_{ij}/(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j))$. Since $\mathbf{Z} \in \mathcal{T}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$,  $\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}$ is doubly stochastic, and the van der Waerden lower bound for permanents of doubly stochastic matrices \cite{Falikman1981vanderwaerden} gives $\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}) \geq N!/N^{N}$. Since $\widetilde{\mathbf{Z}}$ is obtained from $\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}$ by multiplying the rows of the $i$th block row by $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)$ and the columns of the $j$th block column by $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)$,
		\begin{align}
			\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\mathbf{Z}}) = \prod_{i = 1}^{m}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)} \prod_{j = 1}^{n} \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}\,\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}) \geq \frac{N!}{N^{N}}\prod_{i = 1}^{m}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)} \prod_{j = 1}^{n} \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}.
		\end{align}
		Combining this with \eqref{eq: ind_model_lower_bd_pf_1} and using $\sum_i \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i) + \sum_j\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j) = 2N$ and $N! \ge (N/e)^N$, we obtain
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{Y} \in \mathrm{CT}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}, \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})\big) &\geq e^{-N}\frac{N!}{N^{N}} \prod_{i = 1}^{m} \frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!}\prod_{j = 1}^{n} \frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}
			= \frac{e^{N}N!}{N^{N}} \prod_{i = 1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!}\prod_{j = 1}^{n} \frac{e^{-\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!} \\
			& \geq \prod_{i = 1}^{m}\frac{e^{-\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!}\prod_{j = 1}^{n} \frac{e^{-\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}.
		\end{align}
		By Stirling's bound $k! \le e\,k^{k+1}e^{-k}$, valid for all $k \ge 1$, we have $e^{-k}k^k/k! \geq 1/(ek)$. Hence the last display is at least $e^{-(m+n)}\prod_{i}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{-1}\prod_{j}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{-1}$. Finally, by the AM--GM inequality, $\prod_{i} \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i) \le (N/m)^m$ and $\prod_{j} \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j) \le (N/n)^n$, which yields the required lower bound.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem: pythagorean}
		Let $c \in L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$ and let $\pi^* \in \Pi(\mu,\nu)$ be of the form $d\pi^* = e^{a\oplus b - c}\,d(\mu\otimes\nu)$ for some $a \in L^\infty(\mu)$ and $b \in L^\infty(\nu)$. Then for every $\pi \in \Pi(\mu,\nu)$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: pythagorean}
			\int c\, d\pi + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi \Vert \mu \otimes\nu) = \int c\,d\pi^* + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi^*\Vert \mu\otimes\nu) + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi \Vert \pi^*).
		\end{align}
		In particular, $\pi^* = \pi^c_{\mu,\nu}$ is the unique minimizer of \eqref{eq: EOT_Problem_General}, and $\Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c) = \int a\,d\mu + \int b\,d\nu$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Since $\frac{d\pi^*}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}$ is bounded above and below by positive constants, $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert\mu\otimes\nu) = \infty$ if and only if $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi \Vert \pi^*) = \infty$, in which case both sides of \eqref{eq: pythagorean} are infinite. Otherwise $\pi \ll \pi^*$ and $\log\frac{d\pi}{d\pi^*} = \log\frac{d\pi}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)} - a(x) - b(y) + c(x,y)$ holds $\pi$-a.s. Integrating with respect to $\pi$ and using that $\pi$ has marginals $\mu$ and $\nu$, we get
		\begin{align}
			D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert \pi^*) = D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi \Vert \mu\otimes\nu) + \int c\,d\pi - \int a\,d\mu - \int b\,d\nu.
		\end{align}
		Applying this with $\pi = \pi^*$ gives $\int c\,d\pi^* + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi^* \Vert\mu\otimes\nu) = \int a\,d\mu + \int b\,d\nu$, and subtracting the two identities yields \eqref{eq: pythagorean}. Since $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert\pi^*) > 0$ for $\pi \ne \pi^*$, the minimizer is unique.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem: density_bounds}
		For every $c \in L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$,
		\begin{align}
			e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(c)} \le \frac{d\pi^c_{\mu,\nu}}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)} \le e^{2\operatorname{osc}(c)} \qquad (\mu\otimes\nu)\text{-a.e.}
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $\xi := e^{a\oplus b - c}$ with Schr\"odinger potentials $(a,b)$, and set $B := \int e^{b}\,d\nu$. By the first equation in \eqref{eq: schrodinger_system}, $e^{-a(x)} = \int e^{b(y) - c(x,y)}\,\nu(dy)$ lies between $e^{-\operatorname{ess\,sup} c}B$ and $e^{-\operatorname{ess\,inf} c}B$. Hence
		\begin{align}
			e^{-\operatorname{osc}(c)}\, \frac{e^{b(y)}}{B} \le \xi(x,y) = e^{a(x)}e^{b(y)}e^{-c(x,y)} \le e^{\operatorname{osc}(c)}\,\frac{e^{b(y)}}{B}.
		\end{align}
		Integrating in $x$ with respect to $\mu$ and using the second equation in \eqref{eq: schrodinger_system} shows that $e^{-\operatorname{osc}(c)} \le e^{b(y)}/B \le e^{\operatorname{osc}(c)}$ for $\nu$-a.e.\ $y$. Combining this with the display proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def: sum_space}
		The space of additively separable functions is
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: def_sum_space}
			\mathcal{A} := \big\{ a \oplus b \,:\, a \in L^2(\mu),\; b \in L^2(\nu) \big\}.
		\end{align}
		For $\pi \in \Pi(\mu,\nu)$, we have $\mathcal{A} \subset L^2(\pi)$, and we let $P_\pi$ denote the orthogonal projection of $L^2(\pi)$ onto the closure of $\mathcal{A}$ in $L^2(\pi)$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem: sum_space}
		Let $\pi \in \Pi(\mu,\nu)$ satisfy $c_0 \le \frac{d\pi}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)} \le C_0$ for some constants $0 < c_0 \le C_0 < \infty$. Then $\mathcal{A}$ is a closed subspace of $L^2(\pi)$, so that $P_\pi$ is the orthogonal projection onto $\mathcal{A}$, and for $h \in L^2(\pi)$, $P_\pi h$ is the unique $w \in \mathcal{A}$ such that
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: normal_equations}
			\mathbb{E}_\pi[h - w \mid X] = 0 \quad\text{and}\quad \mathbb{E}_\pi[h - w \mid Y] = 0 \qquad \pi\text{-a.s.},
		\end{align}
		where $(X,Y) \sim \pi$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The norms of $L^2(\pi)$ and $L^2(\mu\otimes\nu)$ are equivalent, so it suffices to show that $\mathcal{A}$ is closed in $L^2(\mu\otimes\nu)$. There, $\mathcal{A} = \mathbb{R} \oplus L^2_0(\mu) \oplus L^2_0(\nu)$ is an orthogonal direct sum of closed subspaces, where $L^2_0$ denotes mean-zero functions of one coordinate; hence $\mathcal{A}$ is closed. Since $\mathcal{A}$ is closed, $P_\pi h$ is the unique $w \in \mathcal{A}$ such that $h - w$ is orthogonal in $L^2(\pi)$ to $a(X)$ and $b(Y)$ for all $a \in L^2(\mu)$ and $b \in L^2(\nu)$. Since $X \sim \mu$ under $\pi$, the functions $a(X)$, $a \in L^2(\mu)$, are the $\sigma(X)$-measurable elements of $L^2(\pi)$, onto which $\mathbb{E}_\pi[\,\cdot\mid X]$ is the orthogonal projection; hence $h - w \perp a(X)$ for all $a$ if and only if $\mathbb{E}_\pi[h - w \mid X] = 0$, and similarly for $Y$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem: typical_table_bridge}
		Let $\mathbf{Z} = \mathbf{Z}^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}$ solve \eqref{eq: def_matrix_scaling}. Then $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}} = \pi^{\kappa}_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}}$ and
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: typical_table_density}
			\frac{d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}}}{d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}})} = \frac{N \mathbf{Z}_{ij}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)} \quad \text{on } A_{ij}, \qquad \Gamma_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}}(\kappa) = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i,j} \mathbf{Z}_{ij}\log\frac{\mathbf{Z}_{ij}\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1}{N\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}}.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Since $\norm{\mathbf{Z}}_1 = N$, we have $\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}} = mn\mathbf{Z}_{ij}/N$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(x)\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(y) = mn\,\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)/N^2$ on $A_{ij}$, which gives the first formula in \eqref{eq: typical_table_density}. By \eqref{eq: typical_table_tilting} and \eqref{eq: def_kappa}, on $A_{ij}$,
		\begin{align}
			\frac{N \mathbf{Z}_{ij}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)} = \frac{N\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}e^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)+\boldsymbol{\beta}(j)} = \exp\Big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i) + \log\frac{\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1}{N} + \boldsymbol{\beta}(j) - \kappa(x,y)\Big),
		\end{align}
		so $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}}$ has the form required in Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean} with bounded potentials. Since $\mathbf{Z} \in \mathcal{T}(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$, $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}} \in \Pi(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}})$, and Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean} shows that $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}}$ is the minimizer. The formula for the value follows from \eqref{eq: EOT_is_SB}, because $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}}$ and $\pi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$ assign masses $\mathbf{Z}_{ij}/N$ and $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}/\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1$ to $A_{ij}$ and both have grid-step densities.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem: COV_permuton_to_EOT}
		Let $\rho_1, \rho_2$ be probability measures on $[0,1]$, $T = (q_{\rho_1},q_{\rho_2})$, and $c \in L^\infty(\rho_1 \otimes \rho_2)$. Then
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: pushforward_EOT_equivalence}
			\min_{\mu \in \mathcal{M}} \left[ \int_{[0, 1]^2} c \circ T \, d\mu + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u) \right] =  \min_{\pi \in \Pi(\rho_1, \rho_2)} \left[ \int_{[0, 1]^2} c \, d\pi + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi \Vert \rho_1 \otimes \rho_2) \right].
		\end{align}
		Moreover, if $\mu^*$ and $\pi^*$ denote the minimizers of the left- and right-hand sides, then $\pi^* = T_{\#}\mu^*$ and $\frac{d\mu^*}{du} = \xi \circ T$, where $\xi := \frac{d\pi^*}{d(\rho_1\otimes\rho_2)}$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Since $T_{\#}u = \rho_1\otimes\rho_2$, the function $c \circ T$ is well defined $u$-a.e.\ and bounded. Let $(a,b)$ be Schr\"odinger potentials for $\pi^*$, so that $\xi = e^{a\oplus b - c}$, and let $\hat\mu$ be the measure with density $\xi\circ T = e^{(a \circ q_{\rho_1}) \oplus (b \circ q_{\rho_2}) - c\circ T}$ with respect to $u$. The set of $x$ for which the first equation in \eqref{eq: schrodinger_system} fails is $\rho_1$-null, so its preimage under $q_{\rho_1}$ is Lebesgue-null. Hence, for Lebesgue-a.e.\ $s$,
		\begin{align}
			\int_0^1 \xi\big(q_{\rho_1}(s), q_{\rho_2}(t)\big)\,dt = \int \xi\big(q_{\rho_1}(s), y\big)\,\rho_2(dy) = 1,
		\end{align}
		and similarly with the roles of the coordinates exchanged. Thus $\hat\mu \in \mathcal{M}$, and $\hat\mu$ has the form required in Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean} for the cost $c \circ T$ and the marginals $(\mathrm{Leb}, \mathrm{Leb})$, with the bounded potentials $a\circ q_{\rho_1}$ and $b \circ q_{\rho_2}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean}, $\hat\mu = \mu^*$ is the minimizer of the left-hand side of \eqref{eq: pushforward_EOT_equivalence}, and the minimum equals $\int a\circ q_{\rho_1}\,d\mathrm{Leb} + \int b\circ q_{\rho_2}\,d\mathrm{Leb} = \int a\,d\rho_1 + \int b\,d\rho_2$, which is the right-hand side by Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean}. Finally, $T_{\#}\mu^* = \pi^*$, since $\int \phi\, d(T_{\#}\mu^*) = \int (\phi\,\xi)\circ T\,du = \int \phi\,\xi\,d(\rho_1\otimes\rho_2)$ for bounded measurable $\phi$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem: permuton_coordinates}
		Let $\rho_1$, $\rho_2$, $T$ and $c$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem: COV_permuton_to_EOT}, let $\mu$ be the optimal permuton for the cost $c\circ T$, and let $\pi := \pi^c_{\rho_1,\rho_2}$. Then $\frac{d\mu}{du}$ takes values in $[e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(c)}, e^{2\operatorname{osc}(c)}]$, and for all $g, h \in L^2(\pi)$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: permuton_coordinates}
			\begin{split}
				P_\mu(h\circ T) &= (P_\pi h)\circ T, \\
				\big\langle(I-P_\pi)g, (I-P_\pi)h\big\rangle_{L^2(\pi)} &= \big\langle(I-P_\mu)(g\circ T), (I-P_\mu)(h\circ T)\big\rangle_{L^2(\mu)}.
			\end{split}
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By Lemmas~\ref{lem: density_bounds} and \ref{lem: COV_permuton_to_EOT}, $\pi = T_{\#}\mu$ and $w := \xi\circ T$ is a density of $\mu$ with respect to $u$, where $\xi$ is a Borel version of $\frac{d\pi}{d(\rho_1\otimes\rho_2)}$ with values in $[e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(c)}, e^{2\operatorname{osc}(c)}]$. In particular, $L^2(\mu) = L^2(u)$ with equivalent norms, and, by Lemma~\ref{lem: sum_space}, $P_\pi$ and $P_\mu$ are the orthogonal projections onto the spaces $\mathcal{A}_\pi \subset L^2(\pi)$ and $\mathcal{A}_\mu := \{a'\oplus b' : a', b' \in L^2(\mathrm{Leb})\} \subset L^2(\mu)$ of additively separable functions. Since $T_{\#}\mu = \pi$ and $(q_{\rho_i})_{\#}\mathrm{Leb} = \rho_i$, the map $U h := h\circ T$ is a linear isometry from $L^2(\pi)$ into $L^2(\mu)$ with $U(\mathcal{A}_\pi) \subset \mathcal{A}_\mu$.
		
		Let $\sigma(T)$ and $\sigma(q_{\rho_i})$ be the $\sigma$-algebras generated by $T$ and $q_{\rho_i}$, and $\mathcal{B}([0, 1]^2)$ the Borel $\sigma$-algebra of $[0, 1]^2$. Since $T^{-1}(B_1\times B_2) = q_{\rho_1}^{-1}(B_1)\times q_{\rho_2}^{-1}(B_2)$ and the Borel rectangles generate $\mathcal{B}([0, 1]^2)$,
		we have $\sigma(T) = \sigma(q_{\rho_1})\otimes\sigma(q_{\rho_2})$. Let $E_T$ denote the conditional expectation given $\sigma(T)$ in $L^2(\mu)$, which is the orthogonal projection onto the $\sigma(T)$-measurable elements. Since $w$ is $\sigma(T)$-measurable, $E_Tf = \mathbb{E}_u[f\mid\sigma(T)]$ for $f \in L^2(\mu)$: indeed, 
		\begin{align}
			\int_A f\,d\mu = \int f\,\1_Aw\,du = \int \mathbb{E}_u[f\mid\sigma(T)]\,\1_Aw\,du = \int_{A} \mathbb{E}_{u}[f\mid\sigma(T)]d\mu
		\end{align}
		for $A \in \sigma(T)$. Now let $a' \in L^2(\mathrm{Leb})$ and $\alpha := \mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Leb}}[a'\mid\sigma(q_{\rho_1})]$. By Fubini's theorem, \begin{align}
			\int_{A_1\times A_2}a'(s)\,u(ds,dt) = \int_{A_1\times A_2}\alpha(s)\,u(ds,dt)
		\end{align} 
		for $A_i \in \sigma(q_{\rho_i})$, and by Dynkin's $\pi$-$\lambda$ theorem, this identity extends to all $A \in \sigma(T)$. Hence $E_T(a'\oplus 0) = \alpha\oplus 0$. By the Doob--Dynkin lemma \cite{kallenberg2002foundations}, $\alpha = a\circ q_{\rho_1}$ for a Borel function $a$ with $\int a^2\,d\rho_1 = \int\alpha^2\,d\mathrm{Leb} < \infty$. Treating the second coordinate in the same way, we obtain $E_T(\mathcal{A}_\mu) \subset U(\mathcal{A}_\pi) \subset \mathcal{A}_\mu$. Since $E_T$ is self-adjoint and maps $\mathcal{A}_\mu$ into itself, it maps $\mathcal{A}_\mu^\perp$ into itself, so $E_TP_\mu = P_\mu E_T$.
		
		Let $h \in L^2(\pi)$. Since $Uh$ is $\sigma(T)$-measurable, $P_\mu Uh = P_\mu E_TUh = E_TP_\mu Uh \in U(\mathcal{A}_\pi)$, say $P_\mu Uh = Ua_0$ with $a_0 \in \mathcal{A}_\pi$. For every $a \in \mathcal{A}_\pi$, we have $Ua \in \mathcal{A}_\mu$ and hence $\langle h - a_0, a\rangle_{L^2(\pi)} = \langle Uh - P_\mu Uh, Ua\rangle_{L^2(\mu)} = 0$. Thus $a_0 = P_\pi h$, that is, $P_\mu Uh = UP_\pi h$, and \eqref{eq: permuton_coordinates} follows because $U$ is an isometry.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm: permutations_LDP}
		Let $\sigma$ be uniformly distributed on $S_{N}$ and let $\mathbb{Q}_{N}$ be the law of $\mu_{\sigma}$ on $\mathcal{M}$. The sequence $(\mathbb{Q}_{N})_{N\ge 1}$ satisfies a large deviation principle on $\mathcal{M}$, equipped with the weak topology, with speed $N$ and good rate function $\mu \mapsto D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u)$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem: Varadhan_generalization}
		Let $(N_k)_{k \ge 1}$ be positive integers with $N_k \to \infty$, let $F_k, F : \mathcal{M} \to [-\infty, \infty)$, $k \ge 1$, be measurable, and let $\ell_k$ and $\ell$ be as above.
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
			\item If $\liminf_{k} F_{k}(\mu_k) \geq F(\mu)$ whenever $\mu_k \to \mu$ in $\mathcal{M}$ and $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u) < \infty$, then $\liminf_{k\to\infty} \ell_k \ge \ell$.
			\item If $\limsup_{k} F_{k}(\mu_k) \leq F(\mu)$ whenever $\mu_k \to \mu$ in $\mathcal{M}$ and $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u) < \infty$, and if there exists $\eta > 1$ such that
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: exponential_UI}
				\limsup_{k \to \infty} \frac{1}{N_k} \log \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}} \big[ e^{\eta N_kF_{k}(\mu_{\sigma})} \big] < \infty,
			\end{align}
			then $\limsup_{k\to\infty} \ell_k \le \ell$.
		\end{enumerate}
		In particular, if $F_k(\mu_k) \to F(\mu)$ whenever $\mu_k \to \mu$ and $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu\Vert u) < \infty$, and \eqref{eq: exponential_UI} holds, then $\lim_{k \to \infty} \ell_k = \ell$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Both parts use the following consequence of a diagonal argument, which relies on the metrizability of $\mathcal{M}$: if $\liminf_k F_k(\mu_k) \ge F(\mu)$ for every sequence $\mu_k \to \mu$, then for every $\delta > 0$ there is an open neighborhood $\mathcal{U}$ of $\mu$ with $\liminf_k \inf_{\nu \in \mathcal{U}} F_k(\nu) \ge F(\mu) - \delta$, and similarly for upper limits.
		
		(i) Let $s < \ell$, choose $\mu^* \in \mathcal{M}$ with $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu^* \Vert u) < \infty$ and $F(\mu^*) - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu^* \Vert u) > s$, and fix $\delta > 0$. By the assumption of (i) and the diagonal argument above, $\mu^*$ has an open neighborhood $\mathcal{U}^*$ with $\liminf_{k} \inf_{\mathcal{U}^*} F_{k} \geq F(\mu^*) - \delta$. Since $\ell_k \ge \inf_{\mathcal{U}^*} F_k + \frac{1}{N_k}\log \mathbb{Q}_{N_k}(\mathcal{U}^*)$, Theorem~\ref{thm: permutations_LDP} gives
		\begin{align}
			\liminf_{k \to \infty} \ell_k \geq F(\mu^*) - \delta - \inf_{\mu \in \mathcal{U}^*} D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u)  \geq  F(\mu^*) - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu^* \Vert u) - \delta > s - \delta.
		\end{align}
		Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ and $s \uparrow \ell$ proves (i).
		
		(ii) Replacing $F_k$ and $F$ by $\max\{F_k,-t\}$ and $\max\{F,-t\}$ preserves the assumptions of (ii), does not decrease $\ell_k$, and replaces $\ell$ by $\max\{\ell,-t\}$, since $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(u\Vert u) = 0$; letting $t \to \infty$ at the end, we may therefore assume that $F > -\infty$. For $\omega < \infty$, let $\mathcal{C}(\omega) := \{ \mu \in \mathcal{M} : D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u) \leq \omega \}$ be a sublevel set of the rate function; it is compact, since the rate function is good. Fix $\delta > 0$. By the assumption of (ii), the diagonal argument above and the lower semicontinuity of $\mu \mapsto D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u)$, every $\mu \in \mathcal{C}(\omega)$ has an open neighborhood $\mathcal{U}_{\mu}$ with
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: pf_varadhan_generalization_2}
			\limsup_{k \to \infty} \sup_{\nu \in \overline{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}}} F_{k}(\nu) \leq F(\mu) + \delta \quad\text{and}\quad  \inf_{\nu \in \overline{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}}} D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\nu \Vert u) \geq D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u)  - \delta.
		\end{align}
		By compactness, there are $\mu_{1}, \ldots, \mu_{L} \in \mathcal{C}(\omega)$ such that $\mathcal{U}_{\mu_1}, \ldots, \mathcal{U}_{\mu_L}$ cover $\mathcal{C}(\omega)$. The set $\mathcal{W} := \mathcal{M} \setminus \bigcup_{l = 1}^{L}\mathcal{U}_{\mu_l}$ is closed and disjoint from $\mathcal{C}(\omega)$, so $\inf_{\mathcal{W}} D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\cdot\Vert u) \ge \omega$. Let $\ell_\eta$ be the left-hand side of \eqref{eq: exponential_UI} and $\eta' := \eta/(\eta-1)$. By H\"older's inequality,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}} \big[ e^{N_k F_{k}(\mu_{\sigma})} \big] &\leq \sum_{l = 1}^{L} \exp\Big(N_k\sup_{\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\mu_l}}} F_k\Big)\,\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}(\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\mu_l}}) + \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}} \big[ e^{\eta N_k F_{k}(\mu_{\sigma})}\big]^{1/\eta} \mathbb{Q}_{N_k}(\mathcal{W})^{1/\eta'}.
		\end{align}
		By Theorem~\ref{thm: permutations_LDP} and \eqref{eq: pf_varadhan_generalization_2}, the exponential growth rate of the $l$th term in the sum is at most $F(\mu_l) + \delta - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu_l\Vert u) + \delta \le \ell + 2\delta$, and that of the last term is at most $\ell_\eta/\eta - \omega/\eta'$. Hence
		\begin{align}
			\limsup_{k \to \infty} \ell_k \leq \max \Big\{ \ell + 2\delta,\ \frac{\ell_\eta}{\eta} - \frac{\omega}{\eta'} \Big\}.
		\end{align}
		Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ and then $\omega \to \infty$ proves (ii).
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem: gibbs_permuton_form}
		The Gibbs measure \eqref{eq: gibbs_intro} satisfies \eqref{eq: permutation_measure_continuous}.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem: quantile_properties}, $\kappa \circ T$ is equal to the constant $\mathbf{K}_{Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k)Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\ell)}$ on $B_{k\ell}$, where $\mathbf{K}_{ij} := \log\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1}{N^2\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}}$ is the value of $\kappa$ on $A_{ij}$. Since $\mu_\sigma$ is uniform on the squares $B_{k\sigma(k)}$, each of mass $1/N$,
		\begin{align}
			N\int \kappa \circ T\, d\mu_\sigma = \sum_{k=1}^{N} \mathbf{K}_{Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k)Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\sigma(k))} = \sum_{i,j} \mathbf{K}_{ij}\mathbf{X}_{ij}(\sigma).
		\end{align}
		Using that $\mathbf{X}(\sigma)$ has row sums $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}$ and column sums $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop: permutation_CT_coupling}(i)), we obtain
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: gibbs_weight_product}
			e^{-N\int \kappa\circ T\,d\mu_\sigma} = \prod_{i,j}\Big(\frac{N^2 \boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}}{\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}\Big)^{\mathbf{X}_{ij}(\sigma)} = \frac{(N^2/\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1)^N}{\prod_{i}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}\prod_{j}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}}\prod_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{X}_{ij}(\sigma)},
		\end{align}
		and $\prod_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}^{\mathbf{X}_{ij}(\sigma)} = \prod_{k=1}^{N}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{k\sigma(k)}$ by \eqref{eq: block_weight}. Hence the right-hand side of \eqref{eq: permutation_measure_continuous} is proportional to $\prod_{k}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{k\sigma(k)}$. Since both sides of \eqref{eq: permutation_measure_continuous} are probability measures on $S_N$, they coincide.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem: convergence_of_pushfowards}
		Let $\rho^{0}_k$ and $\rho^{1}_k$ be probability measures on $[0,1]$ such that $\rho^{0}_k \to \rho^{0}$ and $\rho^{1}_k \to \rho^{1}$ weakly. Let $T_k := (q_{\rho_k^{0}}, q_{\rho_k^{1}})$ and $T := (q_{\rho^0},q_{\rho^1})$. If $\mu_k \in \mathcal{M}$ and $\mu_k \to \mu$ weakly, then
		\begin{align}
			\lim_{k \to \infty} \int_{[0, 1]^2} g \circ T_k \, d\mu_k  = \int_{[0, 1]^2} g \circ T \, d\mu \qquad \text{for every } g \in C([0, 1]^2).
		\end{align}
		Equivalently, the push-forwards $(T_k)_{\#} \mu_k$ converge weakly to $T_{\#} \mu$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Note that $\mu \in \mathcal{M}$, since $\mathcal{M}$ is weakly closed. By the triangle inequality,
		\begin{align}
			\left|\int g \circ T_k \, d\mu_k  - \int g \circ T \, d\mu \right| \leq \left| \int (g \circ T_k - g \circ T)\,d\mu_k \right| + \left | \int g \circ T \, d\mu_k - \int g \circ T \, d\mu \right|.
		\end{align}
		For the second term, the set of discontinuity points of $g \circ T$ has $\mu$-measure zero, so
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: pushfwd_weak_convergence_pf_2}
			\int g \circ T \, d\mu_k \to \int g \circ T \, d\mu
		\end{align}
		by the Portmanteau theorem. To prove this claim, note that the discontinuity points of $g\circ T$ are discontinuity points of $T$. Since $q_{\rho^{0}}$ and $q_{\rho^{1}}$ are non-decreasing, each of them has at most countably many discontinuity points, say $\{x_i\}$ and $\{y_i\}$. The discontinuity set of $T$ is then contained in $D := \bigcup_{i} (\{x_i\} \times [0, 1]) \cup \bigcup_{i}  ([0, 1] \times \{y_i\})$, and $\mu(D) = 0$ as $\mu$ has uniform marginals. This proves \eqref{eq: pushfwd_weak_convergence_pf_2}.
		
		For the first term, recall that $q_{\rho_k^{0}} \to q_{\rho^{0}}$ and $q_{\rho_k^{1}} \to q_{\rho^{1}}$ at every continuity point of the limits \cite[Lemma~21.2]{vandervaart1998asymptotic}, hence Lebesgue-almost everywhere, and therefore in Lebesgue measure. Fix $\varepsilon > 0$. By uniform continuity of $g$, there is $\eta > 0$ such that $|g\circ T_k - g \circ T| < \varepsilon$ outside the set
		\begin{align}
			E_{k} := \{ |T_k - T| \geq \eta\} \subset \big(\{|q_{\rho_k^{0}} - q_{\rho^{0}}| \geq \eta/2\} \times[0, 1]\big) \cup \big([0, 1] \times \{|q_{\rho_k^{1}} - q_{\rho^{1}}| \geq \eta/2\}\big).
		\end{align}
		Since $\mu_k \in \mathcal{M}$,
		\begin{align}
			\mu_k(E_k) \leq \mathrm{Leb}\big(|q_{\rho_k^{0}} - q_{\rho^{0}}| \geq \eta/2\big) +  \mathrm{Leb}\big(|q_{\rho_k^{1}} - q_{\rho^{1}}| \geq \eta/2\big) \to 0.
		\end{align}
		Hence
		\begin{align}
			\left| \int (g \circ T_k - g \circ T)\,d\mu_k \right| \leq \varepsilon + 2\norm{g}_{\infty} \mu_k(E_k) = \varepsilon + o(1).
		\end{align}
		Since $\varepsilon$ was arbitrary, the proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
(i) By \eqref{eq: def_partition_function},
		\begin{align}
			\frac{1}{N_k!}\mathcal{Z}_k(\kappa_k) = \frac{1}{N_k!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{N_k}} \exp \left( -N_k \int \kappa_k \circ T_k\, d\mu_{\sigma} \right) = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}} \left[ e^{ -N_k \int \kappa_k \circ T_k\, d\mu_{\sigma}} \right].
		\end{align}
		Let $F_k(\mu) := -\int \kappa_k \circ T_k\,d\mu$ and $F(\mu) := -\int \kappa_\infty\circ T_\infty\,d\mu$. The moment condition \eqref{eq: exponential_UI} holds trivially, since $|F_k| \le \sup_k\norm{\kappa_k}_\infty < \infty$. If $\mu_k \to \mu$ in $\mathcal{M}$, then
		\begin{align}
			|F_k(\mu_k) - F(\mu)| \le \norm{\kappa_k - \kappa_\infty}_\infty + \Big|\int \kappa_\infty\circ T_k\,d\mu_k - \int \kappa_\infty\circ T_\infty\,d\mu\Big| \to 0
		\end{align}
		by Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence} and Lemma~\ref{lem: convergence_of_pushfowards}. Thus Lemma~\ref{lem: Varadhan_generalization} gives
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: varadhan_limit}
			\lim_{k \to \infty}\frac{1}{N_k} \log \frac{\mathcal{Z}_k(\kappa_k)}{N_k!} = \sup_{\mu \in \mathcal{M}} \left( -\int \kappa_{\infty} \circ T_{\infty}\, d\mu - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u) \right) = -\Gamma_\infty,
		\end{align}
		where the last equality is Lemma~\ref{lem: COV_permuton_to_EOT}. This proves \eqref{eq: log_parition_limit}.
		
		(ii) Let $\mu^*_\infty$ be the minimizer of $\mu \mapsto \int \kappa_\infty\circ T_\infty\,d\mu + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu\Vert u)$ over $\mathcal{M}$, so that $\pi_\infty = (T_\infty)_{\#}\mu^*_\infty$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: COV_permuton_to_EOT}. We first show that $\mu_\sigma \to \mu^*_\infty$ in probability when $\sigma \sim \mathsf{P}_k$. Fix an open neighborhood $\mathcal{U}$ of $\mu^*_\infty$ in $\mathcal{M}$ and let $\widetilde F_k := F_k$ and $\widetilde F := F$ on $\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{U}$, and $\widetilde F_k := \widetilde F := -\infty$ on $\mathcal{U}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: gibbs_permuton_form},
		\begin{align}
			\mathsf{P}_k(\mu_{\sigma} \notin \mathcal{U}) = \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}} \big[ e^{N_k \widetilde F_k(\mu_{\sigma})} \big]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{N_k}} \big[ e^{N_k F_k(\mu_{\sigma})} \big]}.
		\end{align}
		If $\mu_k \to \mu \in \mathcal{U}$, then $\mu_k \in \mathcal{U}$ for all large $k$ and hence $\widetilde F_k(\mu_k) = -\infty$; if $\mu_k \to \mu \notin \mathcal{U}$, then $\limsup_k \widetilde F_k(\mu_k) \le \lim_k F_k(\mu_k) = F(\mu)$. Applying Lemma~\ref{lem: Varadhan_generalization}(ii) to the numerator (the moment condition holds as $\widetilde F_k \le F_k$) and \eqref{eq: varadhan_limit} to the denominator, we get
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: pf_permuton_weak_convergence_2}
			\limsup_{k \to \infty} \frac{1}{N_k} \log \mathsf{P}_k(\mu_{\sigma} \notin \mathcal{U}) \leq \sup_{\mu \in \mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{U}} \big(F(\mu) - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u)\big) - \sup_{\mu \in \mathcal{M}} \big( F(\mu) - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mu \Vert u) \big).
		\end{align}
		The functional $F - D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\cdot\Vert u)$ is upper semicontinuous on $\mathcal{M}$ ($F$ is continuous by Lemma~\ref{lem: convergence_of_pushfowards}), so it attains its supremum on the compact set $\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{U}$. Its unique maximizer over $\mathcal{M}$ is $\mu^*_\infty \in \mathcal{U}$. Therefore the right-hand side of \eqref{eq: pf_permuton_weak_convergence_2} is strictly negative, and $\mathsf{P}_k(\mu_\sigma \notin \mathcal{U}) \to 0$. Since $\mathcal{U}$ was arbitrary, $\mu_{\sigma} \to \mu^*_\infty$ in probability.
		
		Now fix $g \in C([0,1]^2)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: convergence_of_pushfowards} and a diagonal argument, for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there are $k_0$ and a neighborhood $\mathcal{U}$ of $\mu^*_\infty$ such that $|\int g\circ T_k\,d\mu - \int g\circ T_\infty\,d\mu^*_\infty| < \varepsilon$ for all $k \ge k_0$ and $\mu \in \mathcal{U}$. Hence $\int g\circ T_k\,d\mu_\sigma \to \int g\circ T_\infty\,d\mu^*_\infty = \int g\,d\pi_\infty$ in probability. Finally, by Proposition~\ref{prop: permutation_CT_coupling}(i) and (iii), $\mathbf{X}_k$ can be realized as $\mathbf{X}(\sigma)$ with $\sigma \sim \mathsf{P}_k$, and then $|\int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \int g\circ T_k\,d\mu_\sigma| \le \omega_g\big(\sqrt{m_k^{-2}+n_k^{-2}}\big) \to 0$ by Assumption~\ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}. This proves (ii).
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem: Poisson_Berstein}
		Let $\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_d$ be independent centered Poisson random variables, i.e., $\xi_i + \lambda_i \sim \operatorname{Poisson}(\lambda_i)$, and let $\lambda_{\max} := \max_i \lambda_i$. Then for every $a = (a_1, \ldots, a_d) \in \mathbb{R}^d \setminus\{0\}$ and $t \ge 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{P}\left( \Big| \sum_{i = 1}^{d} a_i \xi_i \Big| \geq \norm{a}_2^2\,t \right) \leq 2 \exp \left( - \left( \frac{\norm{a}_2^2t^2}{4\lambda_{\max}} \wedge \frac{3\norm{a}_2^2 t}{4\norm{a}_{\infty}}\right) \right).
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Since $\log \mathbb{E}[e^{s\xi_i}] = \lambda_i(e^s - s - 1) \le \lambda_i s^2/(2(1 - |s|/3))$ for $|s| < 3$, the sum $\sum_i a_i\xi_i$ and its negative are sub-gamma with variance factor $\lambda_{\max}\norm{a}_2^2$ and scale parameter $\norm{a}_\infty/3$. The claim therefore follows from Bernstein's inequality for sub-gamma random variables \cite[Section~2.4]{boucheron2013concentration}, $\mathbb{P}(|\sum_i a_i\xi_i| \ge x) \le 2\exp\big(-x^2/(2(\lambda_{\max}\norm{a}_2^2 + x\norm{a}_\infty/3))\big)$, applied with $x = \norm{a}_2^2\,t$, together with $2(\lambda_{\max} + t\norm{a}_\infty/3) \le 4\max\{\lambda_{\max}, t\norm{a}_\infty/3\}$.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem: weak_concentration_random_hist}
		Suppose that $\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1} \geq c\,mn \norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\infty}$ for some $c \in (0, 1]$. For $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$ and $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$, let $L_g(\mathbf{A}) := \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{G}_{ij}\mathbf{A}_{ij}$ with $\mathbf{G}_{ij} = mn\int_{A_{ij}} g$, so that $L_g(\mathbf{A}) = \int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{A}}$ whenever $\norm{\mathbf{A}}_1 = N$. Then for every $s \ge 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: independence_model_histogram_concentration}
			\mathbb{P} \big( | L_g(\mathbf{Y}) - L_g(\mathbf{Z}) | \geq s \big) \leq 2\exp \left( - \left( \frac{c N s^2}{4 e^{5\norm{\kappa}_\infty}\norm{g}_{L^2}^2} \wedge \frac{3Ns}{ 4\norm{g}_{L^{\infty}}} \right) \right),
		\end{align}
		and
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: conditioned_model_histogram_concentration}
			\mathbb{P} \left( \left| \int g \, d(\pi_{\mathbf{X}} - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}}) \right| \geq s \right) \leq \frac{(eN)^{m+n}}{m^mn^n}\cdot 2\exp \left(- \left( \frac{c N s^2}{4 e^{5\norm{\kappa}_\infty}\norm{g}_{L^2}^2} \wedge \frac{3Ns}{ 4\norm{g}_{L^{\infty}}} \right) \right),
		\end{align}
		where $\kappa$ is the cost induced by $\pi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$ as in \eqref{eq: def_kappa}.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
We have $L_g(\mathbf{Y}) - L_g(\mathbf{Z}) = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i,j} \mathbf{G}_{ij}\bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{ij}$, where $\bar {\mathbf{Y}} := \mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{Z}$ has independent centered Poisson entries with parameters $\mathbf{Z}_{ij} \le \lambda := \max_{i,j} \mathbf{Z}_{ij}$. Lemma~\ref{lem: Poisson_Berstein} with $a = \mathbf{G}$ and $t = Ns/\norm{\mathbf{G}}_2^2$ gives
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{P}\big(|L_g(\mathbf{Y}) - L_g(\mathbf{Z})| \ge s\big) = \mathbb{P} \Big( \Big|\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{G}_{ij} \bar{\mathbf{Y}}_{ij}\Big| \geq \norm{\mathbf{G}}_{2}^2 t \Big) \leq 2 \exp \left( - \left( \frac{N^2s^2}{4\lambda\norm{\mathbf{G}}_2^2} \wedge \frac{3Ns}{4 \norm{\mathbf{G}}_{\infty}}\right) \right).
		\end{align}
		By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\mathbf{G}_{ij}^2 \le mn\int_{A_{ij}}g^2$, so $\norm{\mathbf{G}}_{2}^2 \leq mn\norm{g}_{L^2}^2$; moreover $\norm{\mathbf{G}}_{\infty} \leq \norm{g}_{L^{\infty}}$. Finally, $\frac{N\mathbf{Z}_{ij}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)} \le e^{2\operatorname{osc}(\kappa)}$ by Lemmas~\ref{lem: typical_table_bridge} and \ref{lem: density_bounds}, and $\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}{N\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}} = e^{\kappa_{ij}}\frac{N}{\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1}$ by \eqref{eq: def_kappa}, where $\kappa_{ij}$ is the value of $\kappa$ on $A_{ij}$.
