Since $2\operatorname{osc}(\kappa) + |\kappa_{ij}| \le 5\norm{\kappa}_\infty$,
		\begin{align}
			\lambda = \max_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}\,\frac{N\mathbf{Z}_{ij}}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}\,\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}{N\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{ij}} \leq \norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{\infty}\,e^{5\norm{\kappa}_\infty}\frac{N}{\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1} \le \frac{e^{5\norm{\kappa}_\infty}N}{c\,mn}.
		\end{align}
		Substituting these bounds gives \eqref{eq: independence_model_histogram_concentration}. Since $\norm{\mathbf{X}}_1 = N$, \eqref{eq: conditioned_model_histogram_concentration} follows from \eqref{eq: independence_model_histogram_concentration} and the transference principle (Lemma~\ref{thm: transference}) applied to $h(\mathbf{A}) := \1\big( | L_g(\mathbf{A}) - L_g(\mathbf{Z}) | \geq s \big)$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: hellinger_stability}
		Let $\mu, \nu, \mu', \nu'$ be a.e.\ positive probability densities on $[0,1]$, and $R, R'$ probability densities on $[0,1]^2$ with, for some $M \ge 1$,
		\begin{align}
			M^{-1} \le \frac{R}{\mu\otimes\nu} \le M \quad\text{and}\quad M^{-1} \le \frac{R'}{\mu'\otimes\nu'} \le M \qquad \text{a.e.}
		\end{align}
		Let $\pi$ and $\pi'$ minimize $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\cdot \Vert R)$ over $\Pi(\mu,\nu)$ and $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\cdot\Vert R')$ over $\Pi(\mu',\nu')$. Then
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: hellinger_stability}
			H(\pi,\pi')^2 \le 40\log M\,\big(\norm{\mu-\mu'}_{L^1} + \norm{\nu - \nu'}_{L^1}\big) + 11M^5\,d_H(R,R').
		\end{align}
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Our assumptions imply that all measures are considered absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure with strictly positive densities. Therefore, in the proof, all integrals are taken with respect to the Lebesgue measure and other measures are identified with their densities. We identify Radon-Nykodym derivatives with the ratio of densities.
		
		Let $c := -\log\frac{R}{\mu\otimes\nu}$, so that $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\cdot \Vert R) = \int c\,d(\cdot) + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\cdot\Vert\mu\otimes\nu)$ on $\Pi(\mu,\nu)$ and $\operatorname{osc}(c) \le 2\log M$, and similarly for $c'$. By \eqref{eq: schrodinger_form}, there are bounded potentials with $\pi = e^{a\oplus b}R$ and $\pi' = e^{a'\oplus b'}R'$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds}, $e^{a\oplus b} = e^{c}\frac{\pi}{\mu\otimes\nu} \in [M^{-5}, M^5]$ a.e. Adding a constant to $a$ and subtracting it from $b$, we may assume that $\operatorname{ess\,sup} a = 0$. Then $b \le 5\log M$ and $b \ge -5\log M - a \ge -5\log M$, and hence $a \ge -5\log M - \operatorname{ess\,sup} b \ge -10\log M$. Thus $\norm{a}_\infty \le 10\log M$ and $\norm{b}_\infty \le 5\log M$, and the same bounds hold for $(a', b')$ after the analogous normalization.
		
		For bounded $(\tilde a, \tilde b)$, define the dual functionals
		\begin{align}
			\mathcal{L}(\tilde a,\tilde b) := \int\tilde a\,\mu + \int \tilde b\,\nu - \int e^{\tilde a\oplus\tilde b}R + 1, \qquad \mathcal{L}'(\tilde a,\tilde b) := \int\tilde a\,\mu' + \int \tilde b\,\nu' - \int e^{\tilde a\oplus\tilde b}R' + 1,
		\end{align}
		and let $V := D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert R)$ and $V' := D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi'\Vert R')$.
		By \eqref{eq: SSB_duality},                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
		$\mathcal{L}(\tilde a, \tilde b) \leq V$, with equality at the Schr\"odinger potentials $(a, b)$, and similarly for $\mathcal{L}'$ and $V'$. 
		                                                                                                                
		               
			                                                                                                            
			             
		                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              
		
		Let $\psi(x) = x\log x - x + 1$. Fix bounded $(\tilde a,\tilde b)$ and define $Q := e^{\tilde a\oplus\tilde b}R'$. A direct computation using $\pi' \in \Pi(\mu',\nu')$ gives the duality gap identity
		\begin{align}
			V' - \mathcal{L}'(\tilde a,\tilde b) &= \int \pi' \log \frac{\pi'}{R'} - \int \pi' \log \frac{Q}{R'} + \int e^{\tilde a \oplus \tilde b}R' + 1\\
			&=  \int \pi' \log \frac{\pi'}{Q} + \int e^{\tilde a \oplus \tilde b}R' - \int e^{a' \oplus b'}R'\\
			&= Q \psi\left(\frac{\pi'}{Q}\right) \geq \int \left(\sqrt{\pi'} - \sqrt{Q} \right)^2 = H(\pi', Q)^2
		\end{align}
		               
			                                                                                                                                                                                             
			             
		       
		                                                   
		                             
		where we used $\psi(x) \ge (\sqrt{x}-1)^2$, which follows from $2\sqrt{x}\psi(\sqrt{x}) \geq 0$.
		
		Let $\mathcal{E}(\tilde a,\tilde b) := \mathcal{L}'(\tilde a,\tilde b) - \mathcal{L}(\tilde a,\tilde b)$. Then $V' = \mathcal{L}'(a',b') = \mathcal{L}(a',b') + \mathcal{E}(a',b') \le V + \mathcal{E}(a',b')$ and $\mathcal{L}'(a,b) = V + \mathcal{E}(a,b)$, so that,                              
		\begin{align}
			H(\pi',Q)^2 \le V' - \mathcal{L}'(a,b) \le |\mathcal{E}(a,b)| + |\mathcal{E}(a',b')|.
		\end{align}
		By the bounds on the potentials,
		\begin{align}
			|\mathcal{E}(a,b)| + |\mathcal{E}(a',b')| \le 20\log M\big(\norm{\mu-\mu'}_{L^1} + \norm{\nu-\nu'}_{L^1}\big) + 2M^5\norm{R - R'}_{L^1}.
		\end{align}
		Since $\pi = e^{a\oplus b}R$, we have $H(Q,\pi) \le M^{5/2}H(R',R)$. By the triangle inequality,
		\begin{align}
			H(\pi,\pi')^2 &\le 2H(\pi',Q)^2 + 2M^5 H(R,R')^2 \\
			&\le 40\log M\big(\norm{\mu-\mu'}_{L^1} + \norm{\nu-\nu'}_{L^1}\big) + 4M^5\norm{R-R'}_{L^1} + 2M^5H(R,R')^2.
		\end{align}
		Finally, $\norm{R - R'}_{L^1} \le H(R,R')\,\norm{\sqrt{R} + \sqrt{R'}}_{L^2} \le 2H(R,R')$, and $H(R,R')^2 \le \sqrt 2\, H(R,R')$ because $H(R,R') \le \sqrt 2$ for probability densities. Since $8 + 2\sqrt 2 \le 11$, this proves \eqref{eq: hellinger_stability}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
We use the triangle inequality
		\begin{align}
			\left| \int g\, d(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \pi_\infty)\right| \leq \left| \int g\,d(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}) \right| + \left| \int g\,d(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} - \pi_\infty) \right|.
		\end{align}
		
		By Lemma~\ref{lem: typical_table_bridge} and \eqref{eq: EOT_is_SB}, $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}$ is the Schr\"odinger bridge between $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$ with reference density $R_k := \varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k}$, and $R_k/(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}) = e^{-\kappa_k} \in [M^{-1},M]$. As discussed before Theorem~\ref{thm: conditioned_model_weak_as_limit}, $\pi_\infty$ is the Schr\"odinger bridge between $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ with reference density $\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_\infty}$, and $\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_\infty}/(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}) = e^{-\kappa_\infty} \in [M^{-1},M]$. By Proposition~\ref{prop: hellinger_stability} and $\norm{\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} - \pi_\infty}_{L^1} \le 2H(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k},\pi_\infty)$, the second term is bounded by the second term in \eqref{eq: rate_bound}.
		
		For the first term, let $s_k := \sqrt{(m_k+n_k)^{1+\varepsilon}\log N_k/N_k}$. Since $N_k s_k^2 = (m_k+n_k)^{1+\varepsilon}\log N_k$, $N_ks_k \ge (m_k+n_k)^{1+\varepsilon}\log N_k$ (as $s_k \le 1$), $\norm{\kappa_k}_\infty \le \log M$ and $\norm{g}_{L^2} \le \norm{g}_\infty$, Lemma~\ref{lem: weak_concentration_random_hist} gives
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{P}\left( \left| \int g\,d(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}) \right| \ge s_k\right) \le 2\exp\Big((m_k+n_k)\log(eN_k) - C_0(m_k+n_k)^{1+\varepsilon}\log N_k\Big),
		\end{align}
		with $C_0 := \min\big\{c/(4M^5\norm{g}_\infty^2),\ 3/(4\norm{g}_\infty)\big\}$. For large $k$, the right-hand side is at most $2e^{-CN_ks_k^2}$ with $C := C_0/2$, which proves the theorem.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem: grid_step_identity}
		Let $f$ be a bounded grid-step function. Then $\mathbf{D}_f$ is doubly stochastic, $e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(f)} \le N(\mathbf{D}_f)_{k\ell} \le e^{2\operatorname{osc}(f)}$ for all $k,\ell$, and
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: grid_step_identity}
			\mathcal{D}(f) = \frac{N^N}{N!}\operatorname{per}(\mathbf{D}_f).
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
For $k$ with $Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k) = i$, we have $\sum_{\ell}(\mathbf{D}_f)_{k\ell} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_j \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)\,\xi_{f,ij} = \int \xi_f(x,y)\,\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(dy) = 1$ for $x \in \big(\frac{i-1}{m},\frac{i}{m}\big]$, by the Schr\"odinger system; the column sums are handled in the same way. The entry bounds follow from Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds}. Let $f_{ij}$ denote the value of $f$ on $A_{ij}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: quantile_properties}, $f\circ T = f_{Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k)Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\ell)} = \widetilde{f}_{k\ell}$ on $B_{k\ell}$. Since $\mu_\sigma$ is uniform on the squares $B_{k\sigma(k)}$, each of mass $1/N$, we get $N\int f\circ T\,d\mu_\sigma = \sum_k \widetilde f_{k\sigma(k)}$, and hence $\mathcal{Z}(f) = \operatorname{per}(e^{-\widetilde f})$ by \eqref{eq: def_partition_function}, where the exponential is taken entrywise. Let $(a_i)$ and $(b_j)$ be the values of the potentials, which are constant on strips, and let $\tilde a_k := a_{Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}(k)}$ and $\tilde b_\ell := b_{Q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}(\ell)}$. Then $e^{-\widetilde f_{k\ell}} = \widetilde{\xi_f}_{k\ell}\,e^{-\tilde a_k - \tilde b_\ell}$, that is,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: D_is_scaling}
			\mathbf{D}_f = \frac1N\operatorname{diag}(e^{\tilde a})\,e^{-\widetilde f}\,\operatorname{diag}(e^{\tilde b}).
		\end{align}
		Pulling the row and column factors out of the permanent, and using $\sum_k \tilde a_k = \sum_i \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)a_i = N\int a\,d\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}$ and $\sum_\ell \tilde b_\ell = N\int b\,d\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}$ together with Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean}, we obtain
		\begin{align}
			\mathcal{Z}(f) = \operatorname{per}\big(e^{-\widetilde f}\big) = N^N e^{-\sum_k\tilde a_k - \sum_\ell \tilde b_\ell}\operatorname{per}(\mathbf{D}_f) = e^{-N\Gamma(f)}N^N\operatorname{per}(\mathbf{D}_f),
		\end{align}
		which is \eqref{eq: grid_step_identity} by the definition \eqref{eq: def_normalized_partition} of $\mathcal{D}(f)$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm: mccullogh_permanent_expansion}
		For each $n \ge 1$, let $\mathbf{A}_n$ be a doubly stochastic $n\times n$ matrix. Assume that
		\begin{align}
			\sup_{n} \frac{1}{n^2} \sum_{i,j} \big|n(\mathbf{A}_n - \mathbf{J}_n)_{ij}\big|^{p} < \infty \quad \text{for all finite } p \geq 1, \qquad
			\sup_{n} \norm{\mathbf{A}_n - \mathbf{J}_n}_{\mathrm{op}} < 1.
		\end{align}
		Then
		\begin{align}
			\frac{n^n}{n!}\operatorname{per}(\mathbf{A}_n) = \big(1 + O(n^{-1})\big)\det\big(\mathbf{I} + \mathbf{J}_n - \mathbf{A}_n^{\top}\mathbf{A}_n\big)^{-1/2}.
		\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor: uniform_mccullagh}
		For every $K \ge 0$ and every $\mathbf{A} \in \mathfrak{D}_n(K)$, $\norm{\mathbf{A} - \mathbf{J}_n}_{\mathrm{op}} \le 1 - e^{-K}$. Moreover,
		\begin{align}\label{eq:uniform_mccullagh}
			\lim_{n \to \infty}\ \sup_{\mathbf{A} \in \mathfrak{D}_n(K)} \left| \frac{n^n}{n!}\operatorname{per}(\mathbf{A})\,\det\big(\mathbf{I} - (\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{J}_n)^\top(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{J}_n)\big)^{1/2} - 1\right| = 0.
		\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem: EOT_operator_spectral_radius}
		Let $(\Omega_1,\mu_1)$ and $(\Omega_2,\mu_2)$ be probability spaces and let $K$ be a measurable function on $\Omega_1\times\Omega_2$ with $K \ge \eta$ almost everywhere for some $\eta \in [0,1]$, $\int K(x,y)\,\mu_2(dy) = 1$ for $\mu_1$-a.e.\ $x$ and $\int K(x,y)\,\mu_1(dx) = 1$ for $\mu_2$-a.e.\ $y$. Then the integral operator $\mathcal{K} : L^2(\mu_2)\to L^2(\mu_1)$ with kernel $K - 1$ satisfies $\norm{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{op}} \le 1 - \eta$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
If $\eta = 1$, then $K \equiv 1$ almost everywhere and $\mathcal{K} = 0$. Otherwise, $\bar{K} := (K - \eta)/(1 - \eta)$ is again a nonnegative kernel with both marginal integrals equal to one, and $K - 1 = (1 - \eta)(\bar K - 1)$. For $h \in L^2(\mu_2)$, let $h_0 := h - \int h\,d\mu_2$. Since $\int (\bar K(x,y) - 1)\,\mu_2(dy) = 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathcal{K} h(x) = (1-\eta)\int \big(\bar K(x,y) - 1\big)h(y)\,\mu_2(dy) = (1 - \eta)\int \bar K(x,y)h_0(y)\,\mu_2(dy).
		\end{align}
		By Jensen's inequality, applied to the probability measure $\bar K(x,y)\,\mu_2(dy)$, and Fubini's theorem,
		\begin{align}
			\norm{\mathcal{K} h}_{L^2(\mu_1)}^2 \le (1-\eta)^2\iint \bar K(x,y)h_0(y)^2\,\mu_2(dy)\,\mu_1(dx) = (1-\eta)^2\norm{h_0}_{L^2(\mu_2)}^2 \le (1-\eta)^2\norm{h}_{L^2(\mu_2)}^2.
		\end{align}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The kernel $(i,j) \mapsto n\mathbf{A}_{ij}$ is doubly stochastic with respect to the uniform probability measure on $[n]$ and bounded below by $e^{-K}$. The associated operator of Lemma~\ref{lem: EOT_operator_spectral_radius} is represented by the matrix $\mathbf{A} - \mathbf{J}_n$, and the operator norm with respect to the uniform probability measure on $[n]$ is the Euclidean one. Hence Lemma~\ref{lem: EOT_operator_spectral_radius} gives $\norm{\mathbf{A} - \mathbf{J}_n}_{\mathrm{op}} \le 1 - e^{-K}$. Since also $|n(\mathbf{A} - \mathbf{J}_n)_{ij}| \le e^K$, every sequence $\mathbf{A}_n \in \mathfrak{D}_n(K)$ satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm: mccullogh_permanent_expansion}. If the claim failed, there would be $\varepsilon > 0$, integers $n_1 < n_2 < \cdots$ and matrices $\mathbf{A}^{(l)} \in \mathfrak{D}_{n_l}(K)$ for which the absolute value in \eqref{eq:uniform_mccullagh} is at least $\varepsilon$. Setting $\mathbf{A}_n := \mathbf{A}^{(l)}$ if $n = n_l$ and $\mathbf{A}_n := \mathbf{J}_n$ otherwise, we would obtain a sequence satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm: mccullogh_permanent_expansion} for which its conclusion, rewritten with \eqref{eq: det_rewrite}, fails.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem: partition_fredholm}
		Let $f$ be a bounded grid-step function. Then the nonzero singular values of $\mathcal{S}_f$ coincide, with multiplicities, with those of $\mathbf{D}_f - \mathbf{J}_N$, and are at most $1 - e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(f)}$. For every $K \ge 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\lim_{N\to\infty}\ \sup_{(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}),\,f} \Big|\mathcal{D}(f)\,\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}\big(I - \mathcal{S}_f^*\mathcal{S}_f\big)^{1/2} - 1\Big| = 0,
		\end{align}
		where the supremum is over all positive integer margins $(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$ with total $N$ and all grid-step functions $f$ with $\operatorname{osc}(f) \le K$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem: quantile_properties}, $w_f = N(\mathbf{D}_f)_{k\ell}$ on $B_{k\ell}$. Let $V_N \subset L^2[0,1]$ be the space of functions that are constant on each interval $I^N_k := \big(\frac{k-1}{N},\frac{k}{N}\big]$, with orthonormal basis $\epsilon_k := \sqrt{N}\1_{I^N_k}$, $k \in [N]$. Since the kernel of $\mathcal{S}_f$ is constant on each square $B_{k\ell} = I^N_k\times I^N_\ell$, $\mathcal{S}_f$ vanishes on $V_N^\perp$ and maps $L^2[0,1]$ into $V_N$. For $h = \sum_\ell h_\ell\epsilon_\ell$ and $s \in I^N_k$,
		\begin{align}
			(\mathcal{S}_f h)(s) = \sum_{\ell}\big(N(\mathbf{D}_f)_{k\ell} - 1\big)\frac{1}{N}\sqrt{N}h_\ell = \sqrt{N}\big((\mathbf{D}_f - \mathbf{J}_N)h\big)_k,
		\end{align}
		so $\langle \mathcal{S}_f h, \epsilon_k\rangle = ((\mathbf{D}_f - \mathbf{J}_N)h)_k$. Thus $\mathcal{S}_f$ is represented on $V_N$ by the matrix $\mathbf{D}_f - \mathbf{J}_N$, which proves the first claim; the bound on the singular values follows from $\mathbf{D}_f \in \mathfrak{D}_N(2\operatorname{osc}(f))$ and Corollary~\ref{cor: uniform_mccullagh}. In particular, $\det\big(\mathbf{I} - (\mathbf{D}_f - \mathbf{J}_N)^\top(\mathbf{D}_f - \mathbf{J}_N)\big) = \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}_f^*\mathcal{S}_f)$, and the limit follows from Lemma~\ref{lem: grid_step_identity} and Corollary~\ref{cor: uniform_mccullagh} with $2K$ in place of $K$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop: uniform_partition_bounds}
		For every $K \ge 0$ there exist $C_K < \infty$ and $N_K \in \mathbb{N}$ such that for all positive integer margins $(\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}})$ with total $N \ge N_K$ and all grid-step functions $f$ with $\operatorname{osc}(f) \le K$,
		\begin{align}
			1 \le \mathcal{D}(f) \le C_K.
		\end{align}
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The lower bound is the van der Waerden bound $\operatorname{per}(\mathbf{D}_f) \ge N!/N^N$ \cite{Falikman1981vanderwaerden}, by Lemma~\ref{lem: grid_step_identity}. For the upper bound, let $s_1 \ge s_2 \ge \cdots$ be the singular values of $\mathcal{S}_f$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: partition_fredholm}, $s_1 \le 1 - \eta$ with $\eta := e^{-2K}$, and by Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds}, $\sum_i s_i^2 = \norm{\mathcal{S}_f}^2_{\mathrm{HS}} = \int (\xi_f - 1)^2\,d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}) \le e^{4K}$. Since $-\log(1-x) \le x/(1-x)$ and $1 - s_i^2 \ge 1 - (1-\eta)^2 \ge \eta$,
		\begin{align}
			\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}\big(I - \mathcal{S}_f^*\mathcal{S}_f\big) = \prod_i(1 - s_i^2) \ge \exp\Big(-\frac{1}{\eta}\sum_i s_i^2\Big) \ge \exp\big(-e^{6K}\big).
		\end{align}
		By Lemma~\ref{lem: partition_fredholm}, there is $N_K$ such that $\mathcal{D}(f) \le 2\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}_f^*\mathcal{S}_f)^{-1/2} \le 2e^{e^{6K}/2} =: C_K$ for $N \ge N_K$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem: TV_cost_stability}
		Let $\mu,\nu$ be Borel probability measures on $[0,1]$ and $c, c' \in L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$. Then
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: TV_cost_stability}
			\norm{\pi^c_{\mu,\nu} - \pi^{c'}_{\mu,\nu}}_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \norm{c - c'}_\infty.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Write $\pi := \pi^c_{\mu,\nu}$ and $\pi' := \pi^{c'}_{\mu,\nu}$. Applying \eqref{eq: pythagorean} with the cost $c$ to $\pi'$, and with the cost $c'$ to $\pi$, and adding the two identities gives
		\begin{align}
			D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert\pi') + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi'\Vert\pi) = \int (c' - c)\,d(\pi - \pi') \le \norm{c - c'}_\infty\norm{\pi - \pi'}_{\mathrm{TV}}.
		\end{align}
		By Pinsker's inequality, each term on the left-hand side is at least $\frac12\norm{\pi - \pi'}_{\mathrm{TV}}^2$; see also \cite[Proposition~3.12]{eckstein2022quantitative}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem: operator_HS_convergence}
		In this setting, $\norm{w_k - w_\infty}_{L^2(u)} \to 0$. In particular, the optimal permutons for the costs $f_k\circ T_k$ converge in total variation to the optimal permuton for the cost $f_\infty\circ T_\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Let $(a_\infty,b_\infty)$ be Schr\"odinger potentials for the limiting problem. Since $f_\infty$ is continuous, we may define $a_\infty$ and $b_\infty$ on all of $[0,1]$ through the Schr\"odinger system \eqref{eq: schrodinger_system}, and then they are continuous. Hence $\xi_\infty = e^{a_\infty\oplus b_\infty - f_\infty}$ is bounded and continuous on $[0,1]^2$. Let $\bar\pi_k := \pi_k(f_\infty)$ be the solution of the intermediate problem with the limiting cost and the discrete margins, and let $\bar\xi_k := d\bar\pi_k/d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k})$. By the triangle inequality,
		\begin{align}
			\norm{w_k - w_\infty}_{L^2(u)} \le \norm{(\xi_k - \bar\xi_k)\circ T_k}_{L^2(u)} + \norm{(\bar\xi_k - \xi_\infty)\circ T_k}_{L^2(u)} + \norm{\xi_\infty\circ T_k - \xi_\infty\circ T_\infty}_{L^2(u)}.
		\end{align}
		
		For the last term, the quantile functions of $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$ converge to those of $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ at every continuity point of the latter \cite[Lemma~21.2]{vandervaart1998asymptotic}, hence Lebesgue-a.e., so that $T_k \to T_\infty$ $u$-a.e. Since $\xi_\infty$ is bounded and continuous, the last term tends to zero by dominated convergence.
		
		For the second term, let $(\bar a_k, \bar b_k)$ be Schr\"odinger potentials for $\bar\pi_k$, again defined on all of $[0,1]$ through the Schr\"odinger system. Since $(T_k)_{\#}u = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$, a change of variables gives
		\begin{align}
			\norm{(\bar\xi_k - \xi_\infty)\circ T_k}_{L^2(u)} \le \norm{e^{\bar a_k\oplus\bar b_k - f_\infty} - e^{a_\infty\oplus b_\infty - f_\infty}}_\infty \le e^{\norm{f_\infty}_\infty}\norm{e^{\bar a_k\oplus\bar b_k} - e^{a_\infty\oplus b_\infty}}_\infty.
		\end{align}
		Since $f_\infty$ is continuous on the compact set $[0,1]^2$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k} \to \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ weakly, \cite[Proposition~B.1]{nutz2023stability} shows that $\bar a_k\oplus\bar b_k \to a_\infty\oplus b_\infty$ uniformly. These functions are uniformly bounded, so the second term tends to zero.
		
		For the first term, again by a change of variables and by Lemma~\ref{lem: TV_cost_stability},
		\begin{align}
			\norm{(\xi_k - \bar\xi_k)\circ T_k}_{L^2(u)}^2 = \int\big(\xi_k - \bar\xi_k\big)^2\,d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}) \le C\norm{\pi_k(f_k) - \bar\pi_k}_{\mathrm{TV}} \le C\norm{f_k - f_\infty}_\infty \to 0,
		\end{align}
		where $C := \sup_k\big(\norm{\xi_k}_\infty + \norm{\bar\xi_k}_\infty\big)$ is finite by Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds}, since $\sup_k\operatorname{osc}(f_k) < \infty$. The last claim follows since $\norm{\cdot}_{L^1(u)} \le \norm{\cdot}_{L^2(u)}$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Let $K := \sup_k \operatorname{osc}(f_k) < \infty$, and let $w_k$ and $w_\infty$ be as above.
		
		\emph{Step 1: Reduction to a Fredholm determinant.} By Lemma~\ref{lem: partition_fredholm}, $\mathcal{D}_k(f_k) = (1 + o(1))\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}_k^*\mathcal{S}_k)^{-1/2}$, where $\mathcal{S}_k := \mathcal{S}_{f_k}$ is the integral operator on $L^2[0,1]$ with kernel $w_k - 1$.
		
		\emph{Step 2: Convergence of the operators.} Let $\widetilde\mathcal{S}$ be the integral operator on $L^2[0,1]$ with kernel $w_\infty - 1$. The Hilbert--Schmidt norm of an integral operator on $L^2[0,1]$ is the $L^2(u)$ norm of its kernel, so Lemma~\ref{lem: operator_HS_convergence} gives $\norm{\mathcal{S}_k - \widetilde\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HS}} = \norm{w_k - w_\infty}_{L^2(u)} \to 0$. Hence
		\begin{align}
			\norm{\mathcal{S}_k^*\mathcal{S}_k - \widetilde\mathcal{S}^*\widetilde\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{tr}} \le \big(\norm{\mathcal{S}_k}_{\mathrm{HS}} + \norm{\widetilde\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HS}}\big)\norm{\mathcal{S}_k - \widetilde\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HS}} \to 0.
		\end{align}
		Since the Fredholm determinant is continuous with respect to the trace norm \cite[Theorem~3.4]{simon2005trace}, $\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}_k^*\mathcal{S}_k) \to \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \widetilde\mathcal{S}^*\widetilde\mathcal{S})$.
		
		\emph{Step 3: Identification of the limit.} Let $U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}} : L^2(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}) \to L^2[0,1]$, $h \mapsto h\circ q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}}$, and define $U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}$ analogously. Since $(q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}})_{\#}\mathrm{Leb} = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$, these maps are isometries, and $U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}^*h(y) = \mathbb{E}[h(t) \mid q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}}(t) = y]$ for $t$ uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$. Conditioning on $q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}}(t)$ shows that
		\begin{align}
			(\widetilde\mathcal{S} h)(s) = \int_0^1\big(\xi_\infty(q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}}(s), q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}}(t)) - 1\big)h(t)\,dt = \big(\mathcal{S} U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}^*h\big)(q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}}(s)),
		\end{align}
		that is, $\widetilde\mathcal{S} = U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}\mathcal{S} U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}^*$. Since $U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}^*U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}} = U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}^*U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}} = I$, the operator $\widetilde\mathcal{S}^*\widetilde\mathcal{S} = U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}\mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S} U_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}^*$ has the same nonzero eigenvalues as $\mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S}$. Therefore $\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \widetilde\mathcal{S}^*\widetilde\mathcal{S}) = \operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}^*\mathcal{S}) = \prod_{i}(1 - s_i^2)$. Finally, $\norm{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 = \int (\xi_\infty - 1)^2\,d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}) < \infty$, and $\norm{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{op}} \le 1 - e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(f_\infty)}$ by Lemmas~\ref{lem: density_bounds} and \ref{lem: EOT_operator_spectral_radius}. Thus $s_i \le 1 - e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(f_\infty)}$ and $\sum_i s_i^2 < \infty$, so the product $\prod_i (1 - s_i^2)$ converges to a positive number. Combining the three steps proves \eqref{eq: permuton_partition_limit_statement}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
We drop the index $k$. Summing \eqref{eq: gibbs_weight_product} over $\sigma \in S_N$ and using \eqref{eq: block_weight} and Barvinok's identity \eqref{eq: barvinok_per_identity}, we obtain
		\begin{align}
			\mathcal{Z}(\kappa) = \frac{(N^2/\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1)^N}{\prod_{i}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}\prod_{j}\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}}\,\operatorname{per}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}) = \Big(\frac{N^2}{\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_1}\Big)^N\prod_{i}\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)!}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}(i)}}\prod_{j}\frac{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)!}{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)^{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}(j)}}\,\mathcal{Z}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}};\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}.
		\end{align}
		Since $\mathcal{Z}(\kappa) = N!\,e^{-N\Gamma(\kappa)}\mathcal{D}(\kappa)$ by \eqref{eq: def_normalized_partition}, this reads $\mathcal{Z}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}};\boldsymbol{\Lambda}} = \mathcal{D}(\kappa)\,\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}};\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$ for all margins. Under Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k) \to D_\infty$ by Theorem~\ref{thm: permuton_partition_det_limit} applied with $f_k = \kappa_k$, which proves \eqref{eq: enumeration_identity}. Since $\mathcal{D}(\kappa) \ge 1$ by the van der Waerden bound (see the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop: uniform_partition_bounds}), we also have $\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}};\boldsymbol{\Lambda}} \le \mathcal{Z}_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}},\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}};\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}$ for all margins.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem: laplace_identity}
		For every $k$, $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$ and $t \in \mathbb{R}$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: laplace_identity}
			\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{t\Phi_k(g)}\big] = \log\frac{\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k + f)}{\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k)} - N_k\Big[\Gamma_k(\kappa_k + f) - \Gamma_k(\kappa_k) - \int f\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}\Big].
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We drop the index $k$. By \eqref{eq:coupling_Gibbs}, $\mathbf{X} \overset{d}{=} \mathbf{X}(\sigma)$ with $\sigma \sim \mathsf{P}$, and $t\sqrt{N}\int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{X}(\sigma)} = t\sqrt{N}\int(\mathsf{E} g)\circ T\,d\mu_\sigma = -N\int f\circ T\,d\mu_\sigma$ by Proposition~\ref{prop: permutation_CT_coupling}(ii). Hence
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(t\sqrt{N}\int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{X}}\Big)\Big] = \frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}(\kappa)}\sum_{\sigma\in S_N}e^{-N\int (\kappa + f)\circ T\,d\mu_\sigma} = \frac{\mathcal{Z}(\kappa + f)}{\mathcal{Z}(\kappa)}.
		\end{align}
		By \eqref{eq: cell_average_invariance}, $t\sqrt{N}\int g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}} = t\sqrt N\int \mathsf{E} g\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}} = -N\int f\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}}$. Therefore $\log \mathbb{E}[e^{t\Phi(g)}] = \log\mathcal{Z}(\kappa+f) - \log\mathcal{Z}(\kappa) + N\int f\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}}$, and \eqref{eq: laplace_identity} follows by substituting $\mathcal{Z}(h) = N!\,e^{-N\Gamma(h)}\mathcal{D}(h)$ for $h = \kappa$ and $h = \kappa + f$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem: quadratic_lower_bound}
		For probability measures $\mu,\nu$ on $[0,1]$ and $c, f \in L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$,
		\begin{align}
			\Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c + f) - \Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c) - \int f\,d\pi^{c}_{\mu,\nu} \ge -\frac{e^{\norm{f}_\infty}}{2}\int f^2\,d\pi^c_{\mu,\nu}.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Since $\int (c+f)\,d\pi + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert\mu\otimes\nu) = D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi \Vert e^{-c-f}(\mu\otimes\nu))$ for $\pi \in \Pi(\mu,\nu)$, the duality \eqref{eq: SSB_duality} with $h := c + f$ gives
		\begin{align}
			\Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c+f) \ge \int a\,d\mu + \int b\,d\nu - \int e^{a\oplus b - c - f}\,d(\mu\otimes\nu) + 1
		\end{align}
		for all bounded $a$ and $b$.
		Choosing for $(a,b)$ the Schr\"odinger potentials of $c$, for which $e^{a\oplus b - c}\,d(\mu\otimes\nu) = d\pi^c_{\mu,\nu}$ and $\int a\,d\mu + \int b\,d\nu = \Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c)$, gives $\Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c+f) \ge \Gamma_{\mu,\nu}(c) - \int(e^{-f} - 1)\,d\pi^c_{\mu,\nu}$. The claim follows from $e^{-y} - 1 + y \le \frac{y^2}{2}e^{|y|}$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{thm: smooth_cost_perturbations}
		Let $f, g \in L^{\infty}(\mu \otimes \nu)$. Then $t \mapsto \Gamma(f + tg)$ is $C^{\infty}$ on $\mathbb{R}$, and for every $t \in \mathbb{R}$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: EOT_derivatives}
			\frac{d}{dt}\Gamma(f+tg) = \int g \, d\pi_t, \qquad \frac{d^2}{dt^2}\Gamma(f+tg) = -\norm{(I - P_{\pi_t})g}_{L^2(\pi_t)}^2.
		\end{align}
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem: variance_continuity}
		Let $\pi_k, \pi$ be probability measures on $[0,1]^2$ with densities $W_k, W$ with respect to $u$ such that $c \le W_k, W \le C$ for constants $0 < c \le C$ and $W_k \to W$ in $L^1(u)$. Let $h_k \to h$ in $L^2(u)$, with $h$ bounded. Then
		\begin{align}
			\norm{(I - P_{\pi_k})h_k}^2_{L^2(\pi_k)} \to \norm{(I-P_\pi)h}^2_{L^2(\pi)}.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
The marginal densities of $\pi_k$ and $\pi$ take values in $[c,C]$. Hence the space $\mathcal{A}$ of additively separable functions is the same closed subspace of $L^2(u)$ for all these measures, and $\norm{(I - P_\pi)h}^2_{L^2(\pi)} = V_\pi(h) := \inf_{w\in\mathcal{A}}\int (h - w)^2W\,du$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: sum_space}; similarly for $\pi_k$.
		
		\emph{Upper bound.} Given $\varepsilon > 0$, since $L^{\infty}(u) \cap \mathcal{A}$ is dense in $\mathcal{A}$, we may choose $w = a\oplus b$ with $a, b$ bounded and $\int (h-w)^2W\,du \le V_\pi(h) + \varepsilon$. Then $V_{\pi_k}(h_k) \le \int (h_k - w)^2W_k\,du$ and
		\begin{align}
			\Big|\int (h_k - w)^2W_k\,du - \int (h-w)^2W\,du\Big| &\le C\norm{h_k - h}_{L^2}\norm{h_k + h - 2w}_{L^2} \\
			&\quad + \Big|\int (h-w)^2(W_k - W)\,du\Big| \to 0.
		\end{align}
		                                                                                                                                                                                                             
		The last term is at most $\norm{h-w}_\infty^2\norm{W_k - W}_{L^1(u)}$, since $h$ and $w$ are bounded. Hence $\limsup_k V_{\pi_k}(h_k) \le V_\pi(h) + \varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon > 0$.
		
		                                                
		               
			                                                                                                        
			             
		                                                                                                               
		
		                                                                                                                                                                           
		
		\emph{Lower bound.} Pass to a subsequence along which $V_{\pi_k}(h_k)$ converges to its $\liminf$  and for which $W_k \to W$ $u$-a.e., and choose $w_k \in \mathcal{A}$ with $\int(h_k - w_k)^2W_k\,du \le V_{\pi_k}(h_k) + 1/k$. The sequence $v_k := h_k - w_k$ is bounded in $L^2(u)$, so along a further subsequence $v_k$ converges weakly to some $\tilde v$ in $L^2(u)$. Then $w_k = h_k - v_k \rightarrow h - \tilde v$ weakly, and $h - \tilde v \in \mathcal{A}$, since closed subspaces are weakly closed. Now for $\varphi \in L^2(u)$,
		\begin{align}
			\int\varphi\sqrt{W_k}\,v_k\,du - \int\varphi\sqrt{W}\,\tilde v\,du = \int\varphi\sqrt W\,(v_k - \tilde v)\,du + \int \varphi\big(\sqrt{W_k} - \sqrt{W}\big)v_k\,du \to 0,
		\end{align}
		because $\norm{\varphi(\sqrt{W_k} - \sqrt W)}_{L^2} \to 0$ by dominated convergence.                           
		Thus $\sqrt{W_k}\,v_k \rightarrow \sqrt{W}\,\tilde v$ weakly, and by weak lower semicontinuity of the norm, $\liminf_k V_{\pi_k}(h_k) \ge \int \tilde v^2W\,du \ge V_\pi(h)$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{prop: exp_moments}
		For all $k$ with $N_k \ge N_U$, all $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$, and all $t\in\mathbb{R}$ with $|t|\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty \le \sqrt{N_k}$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}\big[e^{t\Phi_k(g)}\big] \le C_U\exp\Big(\frac{e}{2}\,t^2\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})}^2\Big).
		\end{align}
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
In \eqref{eq: laplace_identity} we have $\norm{f}_\infty \le 1$, so $\operatorname{osc}(\kappa_k + f) \le K_1$, and Proposition~\ref{prop: uniform_partition_bounds} gives $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k + f) \le C_U$ and $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k) \ge 1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: quadratic_lower_bound} with $c = \kappa_k$ and Lemma~\ref{lem: typical_table_bridge}, the second term in \eqref{eq: laplace_identity} is at most $\frac{N_k}{2}e\int f^2\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} = \frac{e}{2}\,t^2\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}^2_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})}$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor: moments}
		For every integer $p \ge 1$ there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p$ and $K_1$, such that for all $k$ with $N_k \ge N_U$ and all $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}|\Phi_k(g)|^p \le C_p\Big(\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})}^p + N_k^{-p/2}\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty^p\Big) \le C_p\Big(\norm{g}_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})}^p + N_k^{-p/2}\norm{g}_\infty^p\Big).
		\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Let $\varsigma := \norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})}$. If $\varsigma = 0$, then $\mathsf{E}_k g = 0$, since $\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}$ is positive on every cell by \eqref{eq: typical_table_tilting}, and $\Phi_k(g) = \Phi_k(\mathsf{E}_k g) = 0$. Otherwise, let
		\begin{align}
			t := \min\big\{\varsigma^{-1},\ \sqrt{N_k}/\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty\big\}.
		\end{align}
		Using $|y|^p \le p!\,t^{-p}(e^{ty} + e^{-ty})$ and Proposition~\ref{prop: exp_moments} with $\pm t$, we get $\mathbb{E}|\Phi_k(g)|^p \le 2p!\,C_Ue^{e/2}\,t^{-p}$, and $t^{-p} \le \varsigma^p + N_k^{-p/2}\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty^p$. For the second inequality, note that $\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty \le \norm{g}_\infty$ and, by Jensen's inequality and \eqref{eq: cell_average_invariance}, $\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}^2_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})} \le \int\mathsf{E}_k(g^2)\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} = \int g^2\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{prop: cloned_CLT}
		For all $g_1, \ldots, g_p \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$, $(\Phi_k(g_1), \ldots, \Phi_k(g_p)) \Rightarrow (\Xi(g_1), \ldots, \Xi(g_p))$.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Let $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$ and $t \in \mathbb{R}$, and let $s_k := -tN_k^{-1/2}$ and $h_k := \mathsf{E}_kg$, so that $f = s_kh_k$ in Lemma~\ref{lem: laplace_identity}. For bounded costs $c$, we write $\pi(c) := \pi^c_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_0},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_0}}$ and $\xi_c := \frac{d\pi(c)}{d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_0}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_0})}$. We consider only $k$ so large that $|s_k|\norm{g}_\infty \le 1$, so that $\operatorname{osc}(\kappa_0 + \theta h_k) \le K_1 = \operatorname{osc}(\kappa_0) + 2$ for $|\theta| \le |s_k|$.
		
		\emph{The partition function term.} By Lemma~\ref{lem: partition_fredholm}, $\mathcal{D}_k(c) = (1 + o(1))\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}_c^*\mathcal{S}_c)^{-1/2}$ for $c \in \{\kappa_0, \kappa_0 + s_kh_k\}$, where $\mathcal{S}_c$ is the integral operator on $L^2[0,1]$ with kernel $\xi_c\circ T_0 - 1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: TV_cost_stability}, $\norm{\pi(\kappa_0 + s_kh_k) - \pi(\kappa_0)}_{\mathrm{TV}} \le |s_k|\norm{g}_\infty$. Since $(T_0)_{\#}u = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_0}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_0}$ and the densities $\xi_c$ take values in $[e^{-2K_1},e^{2K_1}]$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds},
		\begin{align}
			\norm{\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_0 + s_kh_k} - \mathcal{S}_{\kappa_0}}_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 = \int\big(\xi_{\kappa_0 + s_kh_k} - \xi_{\kappa_0}\big)^2\,d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_0}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_0}) \le e^{2K_1}|s_k|\norm{g}_\infty \to 0.
		\end{align}
		Hence $\mathcal{S}^*_{\kappa_0 + s_kh_k}\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_0 + s_kh_k} \to \mathcal{S}^*_{\kappa_0}\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_0}$ in the trace norm, as in Step~2 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: permuton_partition_det_limit}. Since the Fredholm determinant is continuous in the trace norm, and the nonzero singular values of $\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_0}$ are at most $1 - e^{-2K_1}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: partition_fredholm}, $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_0 + s_kh_k)$ and $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_0)$ both converge to $\operatorname{det}_{\mathrm{F}}(I - \mathcal{S}^*_{\kappa_0}\mathcal{S}_{\kappa_0})^{-1/2} \in [1,\infty)$, and the first term in \eqref{eq: laplace_identity} tends to zero.
		
		\emph{The entropic term.} By Proposition~\ref{thm: smooth_cost_perturbations}, applied with $\mu = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_0}$, $\nu = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_0}$, the cost $\kappa_0$ and the direction $h_k$, Taylor's theorem gives $\theta_k$ between $0$ and $s_k$ such that, with $\pi'_k := \pi(\kappa_0 + \theta_kh_k)$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: taylor_entropic}
			-N_k\Big[\Gamma_k(\kappa_0 + s_kh_k) - \Gamma_k(\kappa_0) - s_k\int h_k\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_0}\Big] = \frac{t^2}{2}\norm{(I - P_{\pi'_k})h_k}^2_{L^2(\pi'_k)}.
		\end{align}
		The densities of $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_0}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_0}$ are bounded above and below by positive constants, since the margins are positive, and hence, by Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds}, so are the densities of $\pi'_k$ and $\pi_\infty = \pi(\kappa_0)$ with respect to $u$, uniformly in $k$. Moreover, $\norm{\pi'_k - \pi_\infty}_{\mathrm{TV}} \le |s_k|\norm{g}_\infty \to 0$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: TV_cost_stability}, and $h_k \to g$ in $L^2(u)$, because the mesh of the grid tends to zero (Section~\ref{sec: laplace_section}). By Lemma~\ref{lem: variance_continuity}, the right-hand side of \eqref{eq: taylor_entropic} converges to $\frac{t^2}{2}V(g)$.
		
		By \eqref{eq: laplace_identity}, it follows that $\mathbb{E}[e^{t\Phi_k(g)}] \to e^{t^2V(g)/2}$ for every $t \in \mathbb{R}$. Convergence of moment generating functions on $\mathbb{R}$ implies convergence in distribution \cite{curtiss1942note}, so $\Phi_k(g) \Rightarrow \mathcal{N}(0,V(g))$. For the joint convergence, note that $\sum_{i}t_i\Phi_k(g_i) = \Phi_k(\sum_i t_ig_i)$ converges in distribution to $\mathcal{N}(0, V(\sum_it_ig_i))$, which is the law of $\sum_i t_i\Xi(g_i)$. The Cram\'er--Wold device completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem: transported_cell_averages}
		Assume \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}.
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
			\item If $g$ is a bounded Borel function on $[0,1]^2$ that is continuous at $(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$-almost every point, then $(\mathsf{E}_kg)\circ T_k \to g\circ T_\infty$ in $L^2(u)$.
			\item Under hypothesis~(ii) of Theorem~\ref{thm: CLT}, $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ are absolutely continuous, and $(\mathsf{E}_kg)\circ T_k \to g\circ T_\infty$ in $L^2(u)$ for every $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
(a) The set $D_g$ of discontinuity points of $g$ is a Borel set with $(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})(D_g) = 0$, so $T_\infty^{-1}(D_g)$ is $u$-null, and $T_k \to T_\infty$ $u$-a.e., as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: operator_HS_convergence}. Let $(s,t) \in (0,1]^2$ be such that $T_k(s,t) \to T_\infty(s,t) \notin D_g$. Since $F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}(0) = F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}(0) = 0$, the point $T_k(s,t)$ lies in $(0,1]^2$, so $(\mathsf{E}_kg)(T_k(s,t))$ is the average of $g$ over the cell of the $m_k\times n_k$ grid that contains $T_k(s,t)$. These cells shrink to the point $T_\infty(s,t)$, at which $g$ is continuous, so $(\mathsf{E}_kg)(T_k(s,t)) \to g(T_\infty(s,t))$. Hence $(\mathsf{E}_kg)\circ T_k \to g\circ T_\infty$ $u$-a.e., and the claim follows by dominated convergence.
		
		(b) By the Dunford--Pettis theorem, a subsequence of $(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k})$ converges weakly in $L^1[0,1]$; its limit is a density of $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$, so $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$, and similarly $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$, is absolutely continuous. For $k \in \mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}$, let $\varrho_k := \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$ be the density of $(T_k)_{\#}u$. Since $\{\varrho_k > M^2\} \subset (\{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k} > M\}\times[0,1])\cup([0,1]\times\{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k} > M\})$, and a single integrable function is uniformly integrable, $\varepsilon_M := \sup_{k \in \mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\int_{\{\varrho_k > M^2\}}\varrho_k\,du \to 0$ as $M \to \infty$. Let $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$, represented by a Borel function with $\sup|g| = \norm{g}_\infty$, let $\eta > 0$, and choose $\tilde g \in C([0,1]^2)$ with $\sup|\tilde g| \le \norm{g}_\infty$ and $\norm{g - \tilde g}_{L^2(u)} \le \eta$. For finite $k$, $\varrho_k$ is constant on the cells, so Jensen's inequality gives, with $\varphi := g - \tilde g$,
		\begin{align}
			\norm{(\mathsf{E}_k\varphi)\circ T_k}^2_{L^2(u)} = \int(\mathsf{E}_k\varphi)^2\varrho_k\,du \le \int \mathsf{E}_k(\varphi^2)\,\varrho_k\,du = \int\varphi^2\varrho_k\,du \le M^2\eta^2 + 4\norm{g}_\infty^2\varepsilon_M,
		\end{align}
		and similarly $\norm{\varphi\circ T_\infty}^2_{L^2(u)} \le M^2\eta^2 + 4\norm{g}_\infty^2\varepsilon_M$. Since $(\mathsf{E}_k\tilde g)\circ T_k \to \tilde g\circ T_\infty$ in $L^2(u)$ by (a), the $\limsup$ of $\norm{(\mathsf{E}_kg)\circ T_k - g\circ T_\infty}_{L^2(u)}$ is at most $2(M^2\eta^2 + 4\norm{g}_\infty^2\varepsilon_M)^{1/2}$. Letting first $\eta \to 0$ and then $M \to \infty$ proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem: transported_cell_averages}(b), the limiting margins are absolutely continuous in case~(ii), so that every $g \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$ defines an element of $L^2(\pi_\infty)$. In both cases, the test functions form a vector space; in case~(i), because the discontinuity set of a linear combination is contained in the union of those of its terms. Fix such a test function $g$ and $t \in \mathbb{R}$, let $s_k := -tN_k^{-1/2}$ and $h_k := \mathsf{E}_kg$, and take $k$ so large that $|s_k|\norm{g}_\infty \le 1$. We follow the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop: cloned_CLT}.
		
		The functions $\kappa_k$ and $\kappa_k + s_kh_k$ are grid-step and converge uniformly to the continuous function $\kappa_\infty$. By Theorem~\ref{thm: permuton_partition_det_limit}, $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k + s_kh_k)$ and $\mathcal{D}_k(\kappa_k)$ both converge to $D_\infty$, and the first term in \eqref{eq: laplace_identity} tends to zero. As in \eqref{eq: taylor_entropic}, with $\kappa_k$ and $\pi_k(\cdot)$ in place of $\kappa_0$ and $\pi(\cdot)$, the second term is equal to $\frac{t^2}{2}\norm{(I - P_{\pi_{k,\theta_k}})h_k}^2_{L^2(\pi_{k,\theta_k})}$ for some $\theta_k$ between $0$ and $s_k$, where $\pi_{k,\theta_k} := \pi_k(\kappa_k + \theta_kh_k)$. Let $\mu_k$ and $\mu_\infty$ be the optimal permutons for the costs $(\kappa_k + \theta_kh_k)\circ T_k$ and $\kappa_\infty\circ T_\infty$. Since $\operatorname{osc}(\kappa_k + \theta_kh_k) \le K_1$ and $\operatorname{osc}(\kappa_\infty) \le K_1$, Lemma~\ref{lem: permuton_coordinates} shows that the densities of $\mu_k$ and $\mu_\infty$ with respect to $u$ take values in $[e^{-2K_1},e^{2K_1}]$ and that
		\begin{align}
			\norm{(I-P_{\pi_{k,\theta_k}})h_k}^2_{L^2(\pi_{k,\theta_k})} = \norm{(I-P_{\mu_k})(h_k\circ T_k)}^2_{L^2(\mu_k)}, \qquad V(g) = \norm{(I-P_{\mu_\infty})(g\circ T_\infty)}^2_{L^2(\mu_\infty)}.
		\end{align}
		Since $\kappa_k + \theta_kh_k \to \kappa_\infty$ uniformly, Lemma~\ref{lem: operator_HS_convergence} shows that the density of $\mu_k$ converges to that of $\mu_\infty$ in $L^2(u)$, hence in $L^1(u)$, and $h_k\circ T_k \to g\circ T_\infty$ in $L^2(u)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: transported_cell_averages}. By Lemma~\ref{lem: variance_continuity}, applied to $\mu_k$ and $\mu_\infty$, the second term in \eqref{eq: laplace_identity} converges to $\frac{t^2}{2}V(g)$. We conclude as for Proposition~\ref{prop: cloned_CLT}.
		
		It remains to prove the last two claims. By Corollary~\ref{cor: moments}, 
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}|\Phi_k(\tilde g_k)|^p \leq C_{p}(\norm{\tilde g_k}_{L^2(\pi_k)}^{p} + o(1))
		\end{align}
		for every integer $p \ge 1$ whenever $\sup_k\norm{\tilde g_k}_\infty < \infty$. If $\norm{g_{i,k} - g_i}_{\infty} \to 0$, then,  applying this with $p = 2$ shows $\mathbb{E}\,\Phi_k(g_{i,k} - g_i)^2 \to 0$, so that $\Phi_k(g_{i,k}) - \Phi_k(g_i) \to 0$ in $L^2$ and the convergence in distribution persists. 
		Moreover, every linear combination $\sum_i t_i\Phi_k(g_{i,k})$ has moments of all orders bounded uniformly in $k$, hence is uniformly integrable together with all its powers, and its moments converge to those of the Gaussian limit. Joint convergence follows as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop: cloned_CLT}.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Throughout the proof, all margins are equal to one, that is, $m = n = N$ and $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}} = \TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}} = \mathbf{1}$. Then $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}} = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}} = \mathrm{Leb}$, $T$ is the identity, the cells $A_{ij}$ are the squares $B_{ij}$, and $\Gamma_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}} = \Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}$.
		
		\emph{The assumptions and \eqref{eq: mallows_partition}.} For $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_N$ as in Corollary~\ref{cor: mallows}, $\lambda_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1};\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_N}$ is the law of the permutation matrix $\mathbf{X}_N$ of $\sigma_N \sim \mathsf{P}^f_N$, and $\pi_{\mathbf{X}_N} = \mu_{\sigma_N}$. By \eqref{eq: def_kappa}, the cost function of $(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1},\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_N)$ is $\kappa_N = f_N + \log(\norm{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_N}_1/N^2)$, which converges uniformly to $f + \log\int e^{-f}\,du$. Hence Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence} hold with $\gamma = 1$, $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty} = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty} = \mathrm{Leb}$ and $\pi_\infty = \pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}$, since adding a constant to the cost does not change the Schr\"odinger bridge. By Lemma~\ref{lem: typical_table_bridge}, $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_N} = \pi^{\kappa_N}_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}} = \pi_N := \pi^{f_N}_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}$. Moreover, $f_N$ is grid-step, converges uniformly to $f$, and is equal to $f(k/N,\ell/N)$ on $B_{k\ell}$. Hence $N\int f_N\,d\mu_\sigma = \sum_{i}f(i/N,\sigma(i)/N)$ and $\mathcal{Z}(f_N) = N!\,L_N(f)$, so that $\mathcal{D}(f_N) = e^{N\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f_N)}L_N(f)$. Theorem~\ref{thm: permuton_partition_det_limit}, applied with $f_k := f_N$ and $f_\infty := f$, gives \eqref{eq: mallows_partition}, since then $\xi_\infty = W$.
		
		(i) Let $f \in C^1([0,1]^2)$ and $h := f - f_N$. By Proposition~\ref{thm: smooth_cost_perturbations} with $\mu = \nu = \mathrm{Leb}$, the second derivative of $t\mapsto\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f_N + th)$ lies in $[-\norm{h}_\infty^2, 0]$, since $\norm{(I-P_\pi)h}_{L^2(\pi)} \le \norm{h}_\infty$ for every probability measure $\pi$. By Taylor's formula,
		\begin{align}
			\Big|\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f) - \Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f_N) - \int h\,d\pi_N\Big| \le \frac12\norm{h}_\infty^2 = O(N^{-2}),
		\end{align}
		since $\norm{h}_\infty \le (\norm{\partial_x f}_\infty + \norm{\partial_y f}_\infty)/N$. As observed at the beginning of Section~\ref{sec: partition_function}, the density $w_N$ of $\pi_N$ is constant on each square $B_{k\ell}$. For $(x,y) \in B_{k\ell}$, the uniform continuity of $\nabla f$ gives
		\begin{align}
			h(x,y) = \partial_x f\big(\tfrac{k}{N},\tfrac{\ell}{N}\big)\big(x - \tfrac{k}{N}\big) + \partial_y f\big(\tfrac{k}{N},\tfrac{\ell}{N}\big)\big(y - \tfrac{\ell}{N}\big) + o(N^{-1})
		\end{align}
		uniformly in $k$, $\ell$, $x$ and $y$. Since $N^3\int_{B_{k\ell}}(x - \frac{k}{N})\,dx\,dy = N^3\int_{B_{k\ell}}(y - \frac{\ell}{N})\,dx\,dy = -\frac12$ and $\sum_{k,\ell}w_{N,k\ell}/N^2 = 1$, where $w_{N,k\ell}$ is the value of $w_N$ on $B_{k\ell}$,
		\begin{align}
			N\int h\,d\pi_N = N\sum_{k,\ell}w_{N,k\ell}\int_{B_{k\ell}}h\,du = -\frac12\int (\partial_x f + \partial_y f)_N\,d\pi_N + o(1),
		\end{align}
		where $(\partial_x f + \partial_y f)_N$ is the grid-step function equal to $(\partial_x f + \partial_y f)(k/N,\ell/N)$ on $B_{k\ell}$. Lemma~\ref{lem: TV_cost_stability} gives $\norm{\pi_N - \pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}}_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \norm{f_N - f}_\infty \to 0$. As $(\partial_x f + \partial_y f)_N \to \partial_x f + \partial_y f$ uniformly, we obtain
		\begin{align}
			\lim_{N\to\infty} N\big[\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f) - \Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(f_N)\big] = -\frac12\int(\partial_x f + \partial_y f)\,d\pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}.
		\end{align}
		The identity $e^{-a(x)} = \int_0^1 e^{b(y) - f(x,y)}\,dy$ holds for every $x \in [0,1]$ by the choice of $a$. Differentiating it gives $a'(x) = \int_0^1 \partial_x f(x,y)\,W(x,y)\,dy$, and hence $\int \partial_x f\,d\pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}} = a(1) - a(0)$; similarly, $\int\partial_y f\,d\pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}} = b(1) - b(0)$. Together with \eqref{eq: mallows_partition}, this proves \eqref{eq: mallows_pal}.
		
		For \eqref{eq: mallows_tripathi}, let $\tilde f := -\log W = f - a\oplus b$, which is continuously differentiable. Since $W$ has uniform marginals, Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean} shows that $\pi^{\tilde f}_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}} = \pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}$, with Schr\"odinger potentials $(0,0)$ and $\Gamma^{\mathrm{u}}(\tilde f) = 0$. Since $\exp(-\sum_i \tilde f(i/N,\sigma(i)/N)) = \prod_i W(i/N,\sigma(i)/N)$, \eqref{eq: mallows_tripathi} is \eqref{eq: mallows_pal} applied to $\tilde f$.
		
		
		
		(ii) Let $\Phi_N$ be the fluctuation process of $\mathbf{X}_N$, so that $\Phi_N(h) = \sqrt{N}\int h\,d(\mu_{\sigma_N} - \pi_N)$ for $h \in L^\infty([0,1]^2)$, and let $g_{\ell,N}(x,y) := g_\ell(\lceil Nx\rceil/N, \lceil Ny\rceil/N)$ for $\ell \in [L]$. Since $g_{\ell,N}$ is grid-step and $\mu_{\sigma_N}$ gives mass $1/N$ to each square $B_{i\sigma_N(i)}$, we have $\Upsilon_N(g_\ell) = N\int g_{\ell,N}\,d\mu_{\sigma_N}$, so that
		\begin{align}
			N^{-1/2}\,\Upsilon_N(g_\ell) - \Phi_N(g_{\ell,N}) = \sqrt{N}\int g_{\ell,N}\,d\pi_N
		\end{align}
		is deterministic. As the $g_\ell$ are uniformly continuous, $\norm{g_{\ell,N} - g_\ell}_\infty \to 0$, so Theorem~\ref{thm: CLT} gives $(\Phi_N(g_{1,N}), \ldots, \Phi_N(g_{L,N})) \Rightarrow (\Xi(g_1), \ldots, \Xi(g_L))$ with convergence of all moments. In particular, $\mathbb{E}\,\Phi_N(g_{\ell,N}) \to 0$ and $\operatorname{Cov}(\Phi_N(g_{\ell,N}), \Phi_N(g_{\ell',N})) \to V(\mathbf{g})_{\ell\ell'}$. Since $N^{-1/2}(\Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g}) - \mathbb{E}\,\Upsilon_N(\mathbf{g}))$ is the centered vector $(\Phi_N(g_{\ell,N}) - \mathbb{E}\,\Phi_N(g_{\ell,N}))_{\ell\in[L]}$, this proves \eqref{eq: mallows_CLT_continuous}.
		
		Finally, if $f$ and $\mathbf{g}$ are Lipschitz, then $|\frac1N\sum_i g_\ell(i/N,\sigma(i)/N) - \int g_\ell\,d\mu_\sigma| \le \sqrt{2}\operatorname{Lip}(g_\ell)/N$ for every $\sigma \in S_N$, since $\mu_\sigma$ is uniform on the squares $B_{i\sigma(i)}$, and $|\int g_\ell\,d\pi_N - \int g_\ell\,d\pi^f_{\mathrm{Leb},\mathrm{Leb}}| \le \norm{g_\ell}_\infty\norm{f_N - f}_\infty \le \sqrt{2}\norm{g_\ell}_\infty\operatorname{Lip}(f)/N$ by \eqref{eq: TV_cost_stability}. Both errors are $o(N^{-1/2})$, so the left-hand side of \eqref{eq: mallows_CLT} differs from $(\Phi_N(g_\ell))_{\ell\in[L]}$ by $o(1)$, and \eqref{eq: mallows_CLT} follows from Theorem~\ref{thm: CLT}.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{prop: limiting_field}
		Assume \ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}. 
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
			\item \emph{(Existence.)} The process $\Xi$ in Definition~\ref{def: SB_white_noise} exists and is almost surely linear.
			\item \emph{(Pillow.)} Suppose that $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ have no atoms. Then $\mathbb{G}$ has a continuous version $\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}$, and $\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}\circ(R,C)$ is a continuous version of $\mathbb{F}$ that vanishes on the boundary of $[0,1]^2$. For arbitrary limiting margins, if $\pi_\infty = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$, then
			\begin{align}
				\label{eq: pillow_covariance}
				\operatorname{Cov}\big(\mathbb{F}(u,v),\mathbb{F}(u',v')\big) = \big(R(u\wedge u') - R(u)R(u')\big)\big(C(v\wedge v') - C(v)C(v')\big).
			\end{align}
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
(a) Let $\zeta$ be white noise with intensity $\pi_\infty$, i.e., the isonormal Gaussian process on $L^2(\pi_\infty)$. Since $L^2(\pi_\infty)$ is separable, we may fix an orthonormal basis $(f_n)$ of it and i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian variables $(\gamma_n)$ on a probability space $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$. Since $\sum_{n} |\langle h, f_n \rangle_{L^2(\pi_{\infty})}|^2 = \norm{h}_{L^2(\pi_{\infty})}^2 < \infty$ for $h \in L^2(\pi_{\infty})$,  the series $\zeta(h) := \sum_n \langle h, f_n\rangle_{L^2(\pi_\infty)}\gamma_n$ converges in $L^2(\Omega)$, and $\mathbb{E}[\zeta(h)\zeta(h')] = \langle h, h'\rangle_{L^2(\pi_\infty)}$. Its partial sums are linear in $h$, so $\zeta(ag+bh) = a\zeta(g) + b\zeta(h)$ almost surely for all $g,h \in L^2(\pi_\infty)$ and $a,b \in \mathbb{R}$. Hence $g \mapsto \zeta((I-P)g)$ is a centered Gaussian process with the covariance in Definition~\ref{def: SB_white_noise}, so $\Xi$ exists. It is almost surely linear since $\zeta$ and $I - P$ are linear; as this property depends only on the finite-dimensional distributions, it holds for every process with the covariance \eqref{eq:covariance_mx_Xi}.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
		               
			                                                                                                                  
			             
		                           
		Since $P$ is an orthogonal projection and $\pi_\infty$ is a probability measure,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: Xi_variance_bound}
			\mathbb{E}\,\Xi(h)^2 = \norm{(I-P)h}^2_{L^2(\pi_\infty)} \le \norm{h}^2_{L^2(\pi_\infty)} \le \sup|h|^2 \qquad \text{for bounded Borel functions } h.
		\end{align}
		
		(b) Let $\square_{u,v} := [0,u]\times[0,v]$. The indicators of $\square_{u,v}$ and $\square_{u',v'}$ differ only on $\big((u\wedge u', u\vee u']\times[0,1]\big)\cup\big([0,1]\times(v\wedge v', v\vee v']\big)$. Since $\pi_\infty$ has marginals $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$, (a) and \eqref{eq: Xi_variance_bound} give
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: pillow_increment}
			\mathbb{E}|\mathbb{F}(u,v) - \mathbb{F}(u',v')|^2 \le \norm{\1_{\square_{u,v}} - \1_{\square_{u',v'}}}^2_{L^2(\pi_\infty)} \le |R(u) - R(u')| + |C(v) - C(v')|.
		\end{align}
		Let $q_R := q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}}$ and $q_C := q_{\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}}$. Since $R$ and $C$ are continuous, $R(q_R(s)) = s$ and $C(q_C(t)) = t$ (Section~\ref{sec: permutons_prelim}), so \eqref{eq: pillow_increment} gives $\mathbb{E}|\mathbb{G}(z) - \mathbb{G}(z')|^2 \le |z - z'|_1$ for $z, z' \in [0,1]^2$. By Gaussianity, $\mathbb{E}|\mathbb{G}(z) - \mathbb{G}(z')|^{6} \le 15\,|z - z'|_1^3$. Since the exponent $3$ exceeds the dimension $2$, the Kolmogorov--Chentsov criterion on the two-dimensional index set \cite[Theorem~I.2.1]{revuz1999continuous} yields a continuous version $\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}$ of $\mathbb{G}$. Next, $q_R(R(u)) \le u$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\big((q_R(R(u)),u]\big) = R(u) - R(q_R(R(u))) = 0$, and similarly for $C$, so that $\1_{\square_{u,v}} = \1_{\square_{q_R(R(u)),q_C(C(v))}}$ holds $\pi_\infty$-a.e. Hence $\mathbb{F}(u,v) = \mathbb{G}(R(u),C(v)) = \widetilde{\mathbb{G}}(R(u),C(v))$ almost surely, so that $\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}\circ(R,C)$ is a continuous version of $\mathbb{F}$. Finally, $\1_{\square_{u,1}} = \1_{[0,u]}(x)$ and $\1_{\square_{1,v}} = \1_{[0,v]}(y)$ belong to $\mathcal{A}$, and $\1_{\square_{u,0}}$ and $\1_{\square_{0,v}}$ vanish $\pi_\infty$-a.e.\ because $R(0) = C(0) = 0$. Thus $\mathbb{F}$ vanishes almost surely at every point of the boundary of $[0,1]^2$, and by continuity, so does $\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}\circ(R,C)$ on the whole boundary, after a modification on a null event.
		
		If $\pi_\infty = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$, then $Pg = \mathbb{E}[g\mid x] + \mathbb{E}[g \mid y] - \mathbb{E}[g]$, where the expectations refer to $\pi_\infty$. Indeed, the right-hand side belongs to $\mathcal{A}$ and satisfies the normal equations \eqref{eq: normal_equations}, by the independence of the coordinates. For $g = \1_{[0,u]\times[0,v]}$, this gives $(I-P)g = (\1_{[0,u]}(x) - R(u))(\1_{[0,v]}(y) - C(v))$, and \eqref{eq: pillow_covariance} follows from Fubini's theorem.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem: conditioning_representation}
		The process $\Xi$ is white noise with intensity $\pi_\infty$ conditioned to have vanishing marginals. Indeed, let $\zeta$ be as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop: limiting_field}(a) and $\mathcal{G} := \sigma(\zeta(a) : a \in \mathcal{A})$. Since $\zeta$ is a linear isometry from $L^2(\pi_\infty)$ into $L^2(\Omega)$ and $\mathcal{A}$ is closed (Lemmas~\ref{lem: density_bounds} and \ref{lem: sum_space}), the closed linear span of $\{\zeta(a) : a \in \mathcal{A}\}$ is $\zeta(\mathcal{A})$. For centered jointly Gaussian families, conditional expectation coincides with orthogonal projection in $L^2(\Omega)$ \cite[Theorem~9.1]{janson1997gaussian}, so $\mathbb{E}[\zeta(g)\mid\mathcal{G}] = \zeta(Pg)$. Hence $\zeta(g) - \mathbb{E}[\zeta(g)\mid\mathcal{G}] = \zeta((I-P)g)$, which is independent of $\mathcal{G}$ and has the law of $\Xi$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{cor: pillow_increments}
		For all $k$ with $N_k \ge N_U$ and all $z = (s,t)$ and $z' = (s',t')$ in $[0,1]^2$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}\big|\mathbb{G}_k(z) - \mathbb{G}_k(z')\big|^6 \le 2C_6|z - z'|_1^3.
		\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Let $\delta := |z - z'|_1$ and $\square_z := [0,s]\times[0,t]$. Since $T_k$ is a homeomorphism of $[0,1]^2$ with increasing coordinates, $T_k(\square_z) = [0,q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}(s)]\times[0,q_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}(t)]$, so that $\mathbb{G}_k(z) - \mathbb{G}_k(z') = \Phi_k(g)$ with $g := \1_{T_k(\square_z)} - \1_{T_k(\square_{z'})}$, and $g\circ T_k = \1_{\square_z} - \1_{\square_{z'}}$. By Lemmas~\ref{lem: COV_permuton_to_EOT} and \ref{lem: typical_table_bridge}, $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} = (T_k)_{\#}\mu$ for a permuton $\mu$. Since $\square_z\,\triangle\,\square_{z'}$ is covered by two strips of widths $|s - s'|$ and $|t - t'|$, the proof of Corollary~\ref{cor: moments} gives
		\begin{align}
			\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}^2_{L^2(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})} \le \int g^2\,d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} = \int\big(\1_{\square_z} - \1_{\square_{z'}}\big)^2\,d\mu = \mu\big(\square_z\,\triangle\,\square_{z'}\big) \le \delta.
		\end{align}
		By Lemma~\ref{lem: quantile_properties}, $T_k^{-1}(A_{ij}) = I_i\times J_j$ for intervals $I_i$ and $J_j$ of lengths $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k(i)/N_k \ge 1/N_k$ and $\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k(j)/N_k \ge 1/N_k$, and $T_k$ maps $I_i\times J_j$ onto $A_{ij}$ by an affine map, since $F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}$ and $F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$ are linear on the corresponding intervals. Hence the value of $\mathsf{E}_kg$ on $A_{ij}$, the average of $g$ over $A_{ij}$, is the average of $\1_{\square_z} - \1_{\square_{z'}}$ over $I_i\times J_j$, that is, $\alpha_i(s)\beta_j(t) - \alpha_i(s')\beta_j(t')$ with $\alpha_i(s) := |[0,s]\cap I_i|/|I_i|$ and $\beta_j(t) := |[0,t]\cap J_j|/|J_j|$. These numbers lie in $[0,1]$, and $\alpha_i$ and $\beta_j$ are $N_k$-Lipschitz. Therefore $\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty \le \min\{1, N_k\delta\}$ and $N_k^{-3}\norm{\mathsf{E}_kg}_\infty^6 \le N_k^{-3}\min\{1,N_k\delta\}^6 \le \delta^3$, and the claim follows from Corollary~\ref{cor: moments} with $p = 6$.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
(i) We first construct $\hat\Xi$ in the cosine basis. By \eqref{eq: Xi_variance_bound}, $\mathbb{E}\,\Xi(e_j)^2 \le \norm{e_j}_\infty^2 \le 4$. Fix $s > 1$. Since $\sum_j (1+|j|^2)^{-s}\,\mathbb{E}\,\Xi(e_j)^2 \le 4\sum_j(1+|j|^2)^{-s} < \infty$, the series $\hat\Xi := \sum_j \Xi(e_j)e_j$ converges in $L^2(\Omega; H^{-s})$, so $\hat\Xi \in H^{-s}$ almost surely. Since the spaces $H^{-s}$ increase in $s$ with continuous inclusions, the limit does not depend on $s$. For $g \in H^s$, the series $\langle\hat\Xi, g\rangle = \sum_j \hat g_j\Xi(e_j)$ converges almost surely by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, and its partial sums are equal to $\Xi$ applied to the partial sums of the cosine series of $g$. Since $s > 1$, the latter converge to $g$ uniformly, so their images under $\Xi$ converge to $\Xi(g)$ in $L^2(\Omega)$. Hence $\langle\hat\Xi,g\rangle = \Xi(g)$ almost surely, and in particular $\hat\Xi$ is a distributional version of $\Xi$. Conversely, let $\hat\Xi'$ be a distributional version of $\Xi$. Since $\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}^2$ is countable, almost surely $\langle\hat\Xi', e_j\rangle = \Xi(e_j) = \langle\hat\Xi, e_j\rangle$ for all $j$. An element of $H^{-\infty}$ is determined by its coefficients $\langle\cdot, e_j\rangle$, so $\hat\Xi' = \hat\Xi$ almost surely.
		
		Next, elements of $H^\infty$ are continuous, so by Theorem~\ref{thm: CLT}, $\mathbb{E}[e^{i\Phi_k(g)}] \to e^{-V(g)/2}$ for every $g \in H^\infty$. The limit is continuous on $H^\infty$, since $V^{1/2}$ is a seminorm with $V(g)^{1/2} \le \sup|g| \le 2\big(\sum_j(1+|j|^2)^{-s}\big)^{1/2}\norm{g}_{H^s}$ for $s > 1$, by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality and $\norm{e_j}_\infty \le 2$, and it is the characteristic functional of $\hat\Xi$, since $\langle\hat\Xi, g\rangle = \Xi(g)$ is centered Gaussian with variance $V(g)$. Since the embedding $H^{s+s'}\hookrightarrow H^s$, which is diagonal in $\{e_j\}$ with singular values $(1+|j|^2)^{-s'/2}$, is Hilbert--Schmidt for $s'>1$, $H^\infty$ is a nuclear Fr\'echet space, and L\'evy's continuity theorem on its dual \cite{fernique1967processus} (see also \cite{mitoma1983tightness}) yields $\Phi_k \Rightarrow \hat\Xi$ in $H^{-\infty}$.
		
		(ii) By Assumption~\ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence}, $N_k \ge \max(m_k,n_k) \to \infty$, so Corollary~\ref{cor: pillow_increments} applies for all large $k$. The processes $\mathbb{G}_k = \mathbb{F}_k\circ T_k$ are continuous and vanish at the origin, since $\mathbb{F}_k(u,v) = \sqrt{N_k}\int_0^u\int_0^v(\varphi_{\mathbf{X}_k} - \varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k})$. Since the exponent $3$ in Corollary~\ref{cor: pillow_increments} exceeds the dimension $2$, Kolmogorov's tightness criterion \cite{kallenberg2002foundations}, applied to $\mathbb{G}_k$ composed with the $1$-Lipschitz nearest-point projection of $\mathbb{R}^2$ onto $[0,1]^2$, shows that $(\mathbb{G}_k)$ is tight in $C([0,1]^2)$.
		
		Now suppose that $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ have no atoms, so that $R$ and $C$ are continuous with $R(0) = C(0) = 0$. By P\'olya's theorem, $\varepsilon_k := \sup_u|F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}(u) - R(u)| + \sup_v|F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}(v) - C(v)| \to 0$. For $h \in C([0,1]^2)$ and $\delta > 0$, let $\omega(h,\delta) := \sup\{|h(z) - h(z')| : |z - z'|_1 \le \delta\}$, and let $\omega_{R,C}(\delta) := \sup\{|R(u) - R(u')| + |C(v) - C(v')| : |u - u'| + |v - v'| \le \delta\}$. Since $\mathbb{F}_k = \mathbb{G}_k\circ(F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k},F_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k})$, we have $\omega(\mathbb{F}_k,\delta) \le \omega\big(\mathbb{G}_k, 2\varepsilon_k + \omega_{R,C}(\delta)\big)$. By the Arzel\`a--Ascoli theorem, the tightness of $(\mathbb{G}_k)$ gives $\lim_{\delta'\downarrow0}\limsup_k\mathbb{P}(\omega(\mathbb{G}_k,\delta') > \eta) = 0$ for every $\eta > 0$, hence $\lim_{\delta\downarrow0}\limsup_k\mathbb{P}(\omega(\mathbb{F}_k,\delta) > \eta) = 0$, and $(\mathbb{F}_k)$ is tight in $C([0,1]^2)$, since $\mathbb{F}_k(0,0) = 0$. By Theorem~\ref{thm: CLT}, applied to the indicators $\1_{[0,u]\times[0,v]}$, which are continuous outside the $(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$-null set $(\{u\}\times[0,1])\cup([0,1]\times\{v\})$, the finite-dimensional distributions of $\mathbb{F}_k$ converge to those of $\mathbb{F}$. Since finite-dimensional distributions determine laws on $C([0,1]^2)$, every subsequential limit of $(\mathbb{F}_k)$ is the law of the continuous version of $\mathbb{F}$ from Proposition~\ref{prop: limiting_field}(b), and $\mathbb{F}_k \Rightarrow \mathbb{F}$.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem: Hs_convergence}
		By the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: functional_limits}(i), for every $s > 1$, $\hat\Xi \in H^{-s}$ almost surely and $\langle\hat\Xi, g\rangle = \Xi(g)$ almost surely for every $g \in H^s$.
