Moreover, $\Phi_k \Rightarrow \hat\Xi$ in $H^{-s}$. Indeed, fix $s_1 \in (1,s)$. By Corollary~\ref{cor: moments} with $p = 2$ and $\norm{e_j}_\infty \le 2$, for $N_k \ge N_U$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{E}\norm{\Phi_k}_{H^{-s_1}}^2 = \sum_j(1+|j|^2)^{-s_1}\,\mathbb{E}\,\Phi_k(e_j)^2 \le 8C_2\sum_j(1+|j|^2)^{-s_1} < \infty.
		\end{align}
		Balls of $H^{-s_1}$ are compact in $H^{-s}$, so Markov's inequality shows that $(\Phi_k)$ is tight in $H^{-s}$. By Theorem~\ref{thm: CLT}, applied to the continuous functions $e_j$, the finite-dimensional distributions of the coordinates $\langle\Phi_k, e_j\rangle = \Phi_k(e_j)$ converge to those of $\hat\Xi$. These determine laws on the separable Hilbert space $H^{-s}$, so every subsequential limit is the law of $\hat\Xi$.
		
		The range $s > 1$ is sharp: if $\pi_\infty = u$, then $\hat\Xi \notin H^{-1}$ almost surely. Indeed, in this case $P e_j = 0$ for $j_1, j_2 \ge 1$, since $\int_0^1 \mathrm{c}_p = 0$ for $p \ge 1$. Hence $\{\Xi(e_j)\}_{j_1,j_2\ge 1}$ are i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian variables. With $a_j := (1+|j|^2)^{-1}$, the series $\sum_{j_1,j_2\ge 1} a_j(\Xi(e_j)^2 - 1)$ of independent centered terms with variances $2a_j^2$ converges almost surely by Kolmogorov's two-series theorem, since $\sum_j a_j^2 < \infty$, whereas $\sum_{j_1,j_2\ge1} a_j = \infty$. Hence $\sum_j a_j\Xi(e_j)^2 = \infty$ almost surely, that is, $\hat\Xi \notin H^{-1}$.
		
		
		This is precisely the regularity of white noise on $[0,1]^2$. A white noise $\mathbb{W}$ with intensity $u$ has i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian coefficients $\langle\mathbb{W}, e_j\rangle$, $j \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}^2$, so the two arguments above show that $\mathbb{W} \in H^{-s}$ almost surely if and only if $s > 1$, the threshold $d/2$ for white noise in dimension $d = 2$. In fact, if $\pi_\infty = u$, then $\mathcal{A}$ is the closed span of the $e_j$ with $j_1 = 0$ or $j_2 = 0$, and $\hat\Xi$ has the law of $\mathbb{W}$ with these coefficients set to zero.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
For every rectangle $B$, $\sqrt{N_k}(\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k}(B) - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}(B)) = \mathbb{F}_k(B)$. The maps $F \mapsto \sup_B|F(B)|$ and $F \mapsto \int F^2$ are continuous on $C([0,1]^2)$ (the former is $4$-Lipschitz), so the limit laws follow from Theorem~\ref{thm: functional_limits}(ii) and the continuous mapping theorem. For the first claim, since $T_k$ is a bijection of $[0,1]^2$, $\sqrt{N_k}\sup_B|\pi_{\mathbf{X}_k}(B) - \pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}(B)| = \sup_B|\mathbb{F}_k(B)| \le 4\norm{\mathbb{F}_k}_\infty = 4\norm{\mathbb{G}_k}_\infty$, which is tight under Assumptions~\ref{assumption: bounded_cost} and \ref{assumption: margins_weak_convergence} by the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: functional_limits}(ii).
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
If $\kappa_\infty = a\oplus b$, then $a$ and $b$ are bounded, since $\kappa_\infty$ is, and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty} = e^{a\oplus b - \kappa_\infty}\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ has the form required in Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean}, so $\pi_\infty = \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$. Then $\xi_\infty \equiv 1$, so $\mathcal{S} = 0$ and $D_\infty = 1$. The formula for $V(g)$ follows from the expression for $P$ in the product case obtained in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop: limiting_field}(b). Since $R$ and $C$ are nondecreasing, $R(u\wedge u') = R(u)\wedge R(u')$, so \eqref{eq: pillow_covariance} coincides with the covariance of $\mathbb{B}(R(u),C(v))$, and these centered Gaussian processes have the same finite-dimensional distributions. If $R$ and $C$ are continuous, then $\mathbb{B}\circ(R,C)$ is continuous, so it has the law of the continuous version of $\mathbb{F}$.
		
		If $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_k = \mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^\top$ has rank one, then by \eqref{eq: def_kappa}, $\kappa_k = a_k\oplus b_k$ is additively separable, and hence $\int \kappa_k\circ T_k\,d\mu_\sigma = \int a_k\,d\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k} + \int b_k\,d\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$ does not depend on $\sigma$, so $\mathsf{P}_k$ is uniform. A function is additively separable if and only if all its rectangle increments $\kappa(x,y) - \kappa(x',y) - \kappa(x,y') + \kappa(x',y')$ vanish, and this property is preserved under uniform limits; hence $\kappa_\infty \in \mathcal{A}$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem: IPFT_Justification_Phi}
		The map $\Theta$ is $C^\infty$ in the Fr\'echet sense, and its partial derivatives with respect to $c$ and to $(a,b)$ are the bounded linear operators
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: cost_to_margins_derivative}
			\partial_c\Theta(c; a, b)g &= \Big( -\int e^{a(\cdot) + b(y) - c(\cdot, y)}g(\cdot, y)\,\nu(dy),\ -\int e^{a(x) + b(\cdot) - c(x, \cdot)}g(x, \cdot)\,\mu(dx) \Big), \\
			\label{eq: potentials_to_margins_derivative}
			\partial_{(a,b)}\Theta(c; a, b)(h_0,h_1) &= \Big(\int e^{a(\cdot) + b(y) - c(\cdot, y)}\big(h_{0}(\cdot) + h_{1}(y)\big)\,\nu(dy), \\
			&\qquad\quad \int e^{a(x) + b(\cdot) - c(x, \cdot)}\big(h_{0}(x) + h_{1}(\cdot)\big)\,\mu(dx) \Big).
		\end{align}
		Moreover, if $\Theta(c; a, b) = 0$, then $\partial_{(a,b)}\Theta(c; a, b) : \mathfrak{X} \to \mathfrak{E}$ is a linear isomorphism.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
All functions involved are bounded: $c \in L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$ by assumption, and $(a,b) \in \mathfrak{X} \subset L^\infty(\mu)\times L^\infty(\nu)$ by the definition of $\mathfrak{X}$. We write $\Theta = \mathcal{I}\circ\exp\circ\,\mathcal{L} - (1,1)$, where $\mathcal{L}(c;a,b) := a\oplus b - c$ is a bounded linear map from $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)\times\mathfrak{X}$ to $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$, $\exp(w) := e^{w}$ is the exponential on $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$, and $\mathcal{I}(\zeta) := \big(\int \zeta(\cdot,y)\,\nu(dy), \int\zeta(x,\cdot)\,\mu(dx)\big)$ is a bounded linear map from $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$ to $L^\infty(\mu)\times L^\infty(\nu)$. Since $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$ is a Banach algebra, the power series $\exp(w) = \sum_{j\ge0}w^j/j!$ converges absolutely in norm, so $\exp$ is $C^\infty$ in the Fr\'echet sense, with derivative $D\exp(w)h = e^{w}h$; indeed, $\norm{e^{w+h} - e^w - e^wh}_\infty \le \frac12 e^{\norm{w}_\infty + \norm{h}_\infty}\norm{h}_\infty^2$. By the chain rule, $\Theta$ is $C^\infty$, and its partial derivatives are obtained by applying $\mathcal{I}$ to $e^{a\oplus b - c}\,\partial\mathcal{L}(c;a,b)$, which gives \eqref{eq: cost_to_margins_derivative} and \eqref{eq: potentials_to_margins_derivative}; their operator norms are at most $2e^{\norm{a}_\infty + \norm{b}_\infty + \norm{c}_\infty}$.
		
		
		Now suppose that $\Theta(c; a,b) = 0$, and let $K := e^{a\oplus b - c} = \frac{d\pi^c}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: density_bounds}, $K \ge \varepsilon := e^{-2\operatorname{osc}(c)}$. Let $\mathcal{K} h(x) := \int K(x,y)h(y)\,\nu(dy)$ and $\mathcal{K}^\top h(y) := \int K(x,y)h(x)\,\mu(dx)$. Then
		\begin{align}
			\partial_{(a,b)}\Theta(c;a,b)(h_0,h_1) = \big(h_0 + \mathcal{K} h_1,\ \mathcal{K}^\top h_0 + h_1\big),
		\end{align}
		and for $(\phi,\psi) \in \mathfrak{E}$ the equation $\partial_{(a,b)}\Theta(c;a,b)(h_0,h_1) = (\phi,\psi)$ is equivalent to
		\begin{align}
			h_0 = \phi - \mathcal{K} h_1, \qquad (I - \mathcal{K}^\top\mathcal{K})h_1 = \psi - \mathcal{K}^\top\phi.
		\end{align}
		The operator $\mathcal{Q} := \mathcal{K}^\top\mathcal{K}$ has the kernel $q(y,y') := \int K(x,y)K(x,y')\,\mu(dx) \ge \varepsilon^2$ with respect to $\nu$, and $\int q(y,y')\,\nu(dy') = 1$. In particular, $\mathcal{Q}$ preserves $L^\infty_0(\nu)$, and for $h \in L^\infty_0(\nu)$ we have $\mathcal{Q} h(y) = \int (q(y,y') - \varepsilon^2)h(y')\,\nu(dy')$, whence $\norm{\mathcal{Q} h}_\infty \le (1 - \varepsilon^2)\norm{h}_\infty$. Thus the restriction of $\mathcal{Q}$ to the Banach space $L^\infty_0(\nu)$ has operator norm at most $1 - \varepsilon^2 < 1$. Hence the Neumann series $\sum_{j \ge 0}\mathcal{Q}^j$ converges in operator norm on $L^\infty_0(\nu)$, its sum is the inverse of $I - \mathcal{Q}$, and $\norm{(I-\mathcal{Q})^{-1}}_{L^\infty_0(\nu)\to L^\infty_0(\nu)} \le \sum_{j\ge0}(1-\varepsilon^2)^j = \varepsilon^{-2}$. Since $\int(\psi - \mathcal{K}^\top\phi)\,d\nu = \int\psi\,d\nu - \int \phi\,d\mu = 0$, the second equation has a unique solution $h_1 \in L^\infty_0(\nu)$, and then $h_0$ is determined by the first. The solution depends boundedly on $(\phi,\psi)$, which proves the claim; see also \cite[Proposition~3.1]{carlier2020differential}.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Differentiating the identity $\Theta(f+tg; a_t,b_t) = 0$ with respect to $t$ gives $\partial_c\Theta\, g + \partial_{(a,b)}\Theta\,(\dot a_t,\dot b_t) = 0$, where the partial derivatives are evaluated at $(f + tg; a_t, b_t)$. By \eqref{eq: cost_to_margins_derivative}, \eqref{eq: potentials_to_margins_derivative} and the identities $\int \xi_t(x,y)\,\nu(dy) = 1$ for $\mu$-a.e.\ $x$ and $\int \xi_t(x,y)\,\mu(dx) = 1$ for $\nu$-a.e.\ $y$, this is
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: schrodinger_derivative_integral_system}
			\begin{split}
				\dot a_t(x) + \int \xi_t(x,y)\,\dot b_t(y)\,\nu(dy) &= \int \xi_t(x,y)\,g(x,y)\,\nu(dy) \qquad \text{for } \mu\text{-a.e. } x,\\
				\dot b_t(y) + \int \xi_t(x,y)\,\dot a_t(x)\,\mu(dx) &= \int \xi_t(x,y)\,g(x,y)\,\mu(dx) \qquad \text{for } \nu\text{-a.e. } y.
			\end{split}
		\end{align}
		Since $\xi_t$ is the density of $\pi_t$, this says that $\mathbb{E}_{\pi_t}[g - \dot a_t\oplus\dot b_t \mid X] = 0$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\pi_t}[g - \dot a_t\oplus\dot b_t \mid Y] = 0$ for $(X,Y) \sim \pi_t$. As $\xi_t$ is bounded away from $0$ and $\infty$ and $\dot a_t \oplus \dot b_t \in \mathcal{A}$, Lemma~\ref{lem: sum_space} gives
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: potential_derivative_projection}
			\dot a_t \oplus \dot b_t = P_{\pi_t} g.
		\end{align}
		By Lemma~\ref{lem: pythagorean}, $\Gamma(f + tg) = \int a_t\,d\mu + \int b_t\,d\nu$, which is $C^\infty$ in $t$ as the composition of a $C^\infty$ map with a bounded linear functional. Integrating the first equation in \eqref{eq: schrodinger_derivative_integral_system} against $\mu$ and using $\int \xi_t(x,y)\,\mu(dx) = 1$, we obtain
		\begin{align}
			\frac{d}{dt}\Gamma(f+tg) = \int \dot a_t\,d\mu + \int \dot b_t\,d\nu = \iint \xi_t\, g\,d(\mu\otimes\nu) = \int g\,d\pi_t.
		\end{align}
		The map $t \mapsto \xi_t$ is $C^1$ into $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$ with derivative $\xi_t(\dot a_t\oplus\dot b_t - g)$, and integration against $g$ is a bounded linear functional on $L^\infty(\mu\otimes\nu)$. Hence, by \eqref{eq: potential_derivative_projection} and since $P_{\pi_t}$ is an orthogonal projection,
		\begin{align}
			\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\Gamma(f+tg) = \int g\,\big(\dot a_t\oplus\dot b_t - g\big)\,d\pi_t = \big\langle g, (P_{\pi_t} - I)g\big\rangle_{L^2(\pi_t)} = -\norm{(I - P_{\pi_t})g}^2_{L^2(\pi_t)},
		\end{align}
		which proves \eqref{eq: EOT_derivatives}.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{prop: spectral_concentration}
		Let $f$ and $R$ be as above. Then for all $t \ge 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^\top} - \mathbb{E}\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^\top}\right| \ge t\right) &\le 2e^{-\psi(t)}, \\
			\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}}^\top} - \mathbb{E}\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^\top}\right| \ge t\right) &\le 2\exp\Big(m\log\frac{eN}{m} + n\log\frac{eN}{n} - \psi(t)\Big),
		\end{align}
		with $\psi$ as in \eqref{eq: def_psi_spectral}.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:LSI_concentration}
		Let $\nu_1, \ldots, \nu_d$ be probability measures on $\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$. Assume that there are $c_1, c_2 > 0$ such that for every $\tau > 0$ and every $g : \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}\to\mathbb{R}$ with $\sup_{x}|Dg(x)|\le \tau$,
		\begin{align}
			\operatorname{Ent}_{\nu_i}(e^g)\le c_1e^{c_2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\nu_i}\big[|Dg|^2e^g\big], \qquad i = 1, \ldots, d.
		\end{align}
		Let $\nu := \nu_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\nu_d$. If $f:\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}$ satisfies, for some $\alpha,\beta > 0$ and all $x\in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}^{d}$,
		\begin{align}
			\sum_{i=1}^{d}|D_if(x)|^2\le \alpha^2 \quad\text{and}\quad \max_{1\le i\le d}|D_if(x)|\le \beta,
		\end{align}
		then for every $t\ge 0$,
		\begin{align}
			\nu\big(f\ge \mathbb{E}_{\nu}[f]+t\big)\le \exp\left(-\frac{t}{2c_2\beta}\log\left(1+\frac{\beta c_2t}{4c_1\alpha^2}\right)\right).
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
It suffices to establish the inequality obtained from the tensorization step, namely $\operatorname{Ent}_{\nu}(e^f)\le c_1e^{c_2\tau}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[|Df|^2e^f]$ with $|Df|^2 := \sum_{i}|D_if|^2$, for every $f$ with $\max_i\sup|D_if| \le \tau$. By the subadditivity of entropy \cite[Proposition~1.4.1]{ane2000inegalites},
		\begin{align}
			\operatorname{Ent}_{\nu}(e^f) \le \sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\big[\operatorname{Ent}_{\nu_i}(e^f)\big] \le \sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\Big[c_1e^{c_2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\nu_i}\big[|D_if|^2e^f\big]\Big] = c_1e^{c_2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\big[|Df|^2e^f\big],
		\end{align}
		where $\operatorname{Ent}_{\nu_i}$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\nu_i}$ act on the $i$th coordinate. The rest of the argument is identical to the proof of \cite[Proposition~11]{bobkov1998modified}.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem: spectral_gradient}
		Let $h \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$ with $\norm{h'}_\infty \le L_1$ and $\norm{h''}_\infty \le L_2$, and let $\eta > 0$. Then for every $\mathbf{B} \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$,
		\begin{align}
			\max_{i,j}\big|F(\mathbf{B} + \mathbf{E}_{ij}) - F(\mathbf{B})\big| \le 2L_1\eta \qquad\text{and}\qquad \sum_{i,j}\big|F(\mathbf{B} + \mathbf{E}_{ij}) - F(\mathbf{B})\big|^2 \le 4(m+n)L_1^2\eta^2 + 4mnL_2^2\eta^4.
		\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
For symmetric matrices $\mathbf{H}, \mathbf{H}'$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \ge \lambda_2 \ge \cdots$ and $\lambda'_1 \ge \lambda'_2 \ge \cdots$, Lidskii's theorem gives $\sum_\ell|\lambda_\ell - \lambda'_\ell| \le \norm{\mathbf{H} - \mathbf{H}'}_{S_1}$, where $\norm{\cdot}_{S_1}$ is the trace norm \cite[Chapter~III]{bhatia1997matrix}. Hence $|\operatorname{tr} h(\mathbf{H}) - \operatorname{tr} h(\mathbf{H}')| \le L_1\norm{\mathbf{H} - \mathbf{H}'}_{S_1}$. The matrix $\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}_{ij}$ has eigenvalues $\pm 1$ and $0$, so $\norm{\eta\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}_{ij}}_{S_1} = 2\eta$, which proves the first bound.
		
		For the second bound, recall that $\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\operatorname{tr} h(\mathbf{H} + \varepsilon \mathbf{H}')|_{\varepsilon=0} = \operatorname{tr}(h'(\mathbf{H})\mathbf{H}')$. With $\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B}) := h'(\eta\widetilde{\mathbf{B}})$, the partial derivatives of $F$ are
		\begin{align}
			\partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B}) = \eta\operatorname{tr}\big(\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})\,\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}_{ij}\big) = 2\eta\,\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})_{n+i,\,j}.
		\end{align}
		Since $\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})$ is symmetric, each entry $\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})_{n+i,j}$ appears twice in $\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})$, and therefore
		\begin{align}
			\sum_{i,j}\partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B})^2 \le 2\eta^2\norm{\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})}_F^2 \le 2(m+n)L_1^2\eta^2.
		\end{align}
		Next, for symmetric $\mathbf{H}, \mathbf{H}'$ with spectral decompositions $\mathbf{H} = \sum_\ell\lambda_\ell u_\ell u_\ell^\top$ and $\mathbf{H}' = \sum_{\ell}\lambda'_{\ell}u'_{\ell}u'^\top_{\ell}$, and a Lipschitz function $g$,
		\begin{align}
			\norm{g(\mathbf{H}) - g(\mathbf{H}')}_F^2 &= \sum_{\ell,\ell'}\big(g(\lambda_\ell) - g(\lambda'_{\ell'})\big)^2\langle u_\ell,u'_{\ell'}\rangle^2 \\
			&\le \operatorname{Lip}(g)^2\sum_{\ell,\ell'}(\lambda_\ell - \lambda'_{\ell'})^2\langle u_\ell,u'_{\ell'}\rangle^2 = \operatorname{Lip}(g)^2\norm{\mathbf{H} - \mathbf{H}'}_F^2.
		\end{align}
		Applying this with $g = h'$ and using $\norm{\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}_{ij}}_F = \sqrt{2}$, we obtain $|\partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B} + \vartheta \mathbf{E}_{ij}) - \partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B})| \le 2\eta\norm{\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B}+\vartheta \mathbf{E}_{ij}) - \mathbf{G}(\mathbf{B})}_F \le 2\sqrt{2}\,\vartheta L_2\eta^2$ for $\vartheta \in [0,1]$. Since $F(\mathbf{B} + \mathbf{E}_{ij}) - F(\mathbf{B}) = \int_0^1\partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B} + \vartheta \mathbf{E}_{ij})\,d\vartheta$, it follows that $|F(\mathbf{B} + \mathbf{E}_{ij}) - F(\mathbf{B}) - \partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B})| \le \sqrt 2L_2\eta^2$, and hence
		\begin{align}
			\sum_{i,j}|F(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{E}_{ij}) - F(\mathbf{B})|^2 \le 2\sum_{i,j}\partial_{ij}F(\mathbf{B})^2 + 4mnL_2^2\eta^4 \le 4(m+n)L_1^2\eta^2 + 4mnL_2^2\eta^4.
		\end{align}
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
Let $\eta := ((m+n)\bar s)^{-1/2}$ and $h(x) := f(x^2)$ for $x \in \mathbb{R}$. Then $h \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$, $h'(x) = 2xf'(x^2)$ and $h''(x) = 2f'(x^2) + 4x^2f''(x^2)$, and both vanish for $|x| \ge R$; hence $\norm{h'}_\infty \le L_1$ and $\norm{h''}_\infty \le L_2$. For $\mathbf{B} \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$, the eigenvalues of $\eta\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}$ are $\pm\eta\sigma_\ell(\mathbf{B})$, $\ell \le m\wedge n$, together with $|m-n|$ zeros, while the eigenvalues of $\eta^2\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^\top$ are $\eta^2\sigma_\ell(\mathbf{B})^2$, $\ell \le m\wedge n$, together with $(m-n)^+$ zeros. Hence, with $F(\mathbf{B}) := \operatorname{tr} h(\eta\widetilde{\mathbf{B}})$,
		\begin{align}
			\label{eq: gram_vs_dilation}
			\sum_{i=1}^m f\big(\lambda_i(\eta^2\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^\top)\big) = \frac12F(\mathbf{B}) + \frac12(m-n)f(0).
		\end{align}
		In particular, $m\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^\top} = \frac12F(\mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{Z}) + \frac12(m-n)f(0)$, and similarly for $\mathbf{X}$.
		
		The entries $\mathbf{Y}_{ij}$ are independent with $\mathbf{Y}_{ij} \sim \operatorname{Poisson}(\mathbf{Z}_{ij})$ and $\mathbf{Z}_{ij} \le C_{\mathbf{Z}}\bar s$, so by \eqref{eq: poisson_MLSI} their laws satisfy the hypothesis of Lemma~\ref{lem:LSI_concentration} with $c_1 := C_{\mathbf{Z}}\bar s$ and $c_2 := 2$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: spectral_gradient}, the functions $\mathbf{B} \mapsto \pm F(\mathbf{B} - \mathbf{Z})$ satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:LSI_concentration} with $\beta := 2L_1\eta$ and $\alpha^2 := 4(m+n)L_1^2\eta^2 + 4mnL_2^2\eta^4 \le (4L_1^2 + L_2^2/\bar s)/\bar s$, where we used $mn \le (m+n)^2/4$. Lemma~\ref{lem:LSI_concentration}, applied to both functions with deviation $2mt$, gives
		\begin{align}
			\mathbb{P}\big(|F(\mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{Z}) - \mathbb{E} F(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z})| \ge 2mt\big) \le 2\exp\left(-\frac{2mt}{4\beta}\log\left(1 + \frac{2mt\,\beta}{2c_1\alpha^2}\right)\right) \le 2e^{-\psi(t)},
		\end{align}
		since $\frac{2mt}{4\beta} = \frac{mt\sqrt{(m+n)\bar s}}{4L_1}$ and $\frac{2mt\,\beta}{2c_1\alpha^2} \ge \frac{2mL_1t}{C_{\mathbf{Z}}\sqrt{(m+n)\bar s}\,(4L_1^2 + L_2^2/\bar s)}$. By \eqref{eq: gram_vs_dilation}, this is the first claim. The second claim follows from the first and Lemma~\ref{thm: transference}, applied to the indicator of the event in the first claim, viewed as a function of $\mathbf{Y}$, since $\log\big((eN)^{m+n}m^{-m}n^{-n}\big) = m\log(eN/m) + n\log(eN/n)$.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem: dyson_continuity}
		Let $C$, $z$, $\theta$ and $W$ be as above.
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
			\item $\mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}$ maps $\mathfrak{B}_z$ into itself, and $\norm{\mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G) - \mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G')}_{L^\infty} \le \frac14\norm{G - G'}_{L^1}$ for all $G, G' \in \mathfrak{B}_z$. In particular, \eqref{eq: dyson_continuum} has a unique solution $G_{W,\theta}(\cdot,z) \in \mathfrak{B}_z$.
			\item If $W$ is grid-step with respect to an $m\times n$ grid, with values $w_{ij}$, and $\theta = m/(m+n)$, and if $(G_i)_{i \in [m]}$ is the solution of \eqref{eq: dyson_discrete} with $\mathbf{S}_{ij} = w_{ij}/(m+n)$, then $G_{W,\theta}(x,z) = G_{\lceil mx \rceil}(z)$ for a.e.\ $x$. In particular, $\int_0^1 G_{W,\theta}(x,z)\,dx$ is the Stieltjes transform of $\nu_{\mathbf{S}}$ at $z$.
			\item If $\theta_k \in (0,1)$ with $\theta_k \to \theta$, and $W_k : [0,1]^2 \to [0,C]$ are measurable functions with $W_k \to W$ in $L^1([0,1]^2)$, then $G_{W_k,\theta_k}(\cdot,z) \to G_{W,\theta}(\cdot,z)$ in $L^1[0,1]$.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
(a) For $G \in \mathfrak{B}_z$, let $a(y) := \theta\int_0^1 W(x',y)G(x')\,dx'$. Then $\operatorname{Im}a \ge 0$ and $|a| \le \theta C/\operatorname{Im}z \le \frac12$, so $|1 + a| \ge \frac12$ and $\operatorname{Im}(1+a)^{-1} = -\operatorname{Im}a/|1+a|^2 \le 0$. Consequently, $u(x) := (1-\theta)\int_0^1 W(x,y)(1 + a(y))^{-1}\,dy$ satisfies $\operatorname{Im}u \le 0$, so that $\operatorname{Im}(z - u) \ge \operatorname{Im}z$. Hence $\mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G) = -(z-u)^{-1}$ satisfies $|\mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G)| \le (\operatorname{Im}z)^{-1}$ and $\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G) = \operatorname{Im}(z - u)/|z-u|^2 \ge 0$. If $G' \in \mathfrak{B}_z$ with corresponding $a'$ and $u'$, then $|(1+a)^{-1} - (1+a')^{-1}| \le 4|a - a'| \le 4\theta C\norm{G - G'}_{L^1}$, so $|u - u'| \le 4\theta(1-\theta)C^2\norm{G - G'}_{L^1} \le C^2\norm{G - G'}_{L^1}$ and
		\begin{align}
			\big|\mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G) - \mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}(G')\big| = \frac{|u - u'|}{|z - u|\,|z-u'|} \le \frac{C^2}{(\operatorname{Im}z)^2}\norm{G - G'}_{L^1} \le \frac14\norm{G - G'}_{L^1}.
		\end{align}
		Since $\mathfrak{B}_z$ is complete for the $L^1$ norm (it is closed in $L^1$, as its defining constraints are preserved by almost everywhere convergence) and $\norm{\cdot}_{L^1} \le \norm{\cdot}_{L^\infty}$, Banach's fixed point theorem applies.
		
		(b) Let $G(x) := G_{\lceil mx\rceil}(z)$, so that $\operatorname{Im}G > 0$. For $x \in \big(\frac{i-1}{m},\frac{i}{m}\big]$, a direct computation gives
		\begin{align}
			(1-\theta)\int_0^1\frac{W(x,y)\,dy}{1 + \theta\int_0^1 W(x',y)G(x')\,dx'} = \sum_{l=1}^n\frac{w_{il}/(m+n)}{1 + \sum_{j=1}^m \frac{w_{jl}}{m+n}G_j(z)} = \sum_{l=1}^n\frac{\mathbf{S}_{il}}{1 + \sum_{j=1}^m \mathbf{S}_{jl}G_j(z)},
		\end{align}
		so $G$ solves \eqref{eq: dyson_continuum}. By the argument in (a), any solution with $\operatorname{Im}G > 0$ satisfies $|G| \le (\operatorname{Im}z)^{-1}$, since the bound $\operatorname{Im}u \le 0$ only uses $\operatorname{Im}G \ge 0$. Hence $G \in \mathfrak{B}_z$, and $G = G_{W,\theta}$ by uniqueness.
		
		(c) Write $G := G_{W,\theta}$, $G_k := G_{W_k,\theta_k}$, $\mathcal{T} := \mathcal{T}_{W,\theta}$ and $\mathcal{T}_k := \mathcal{T}_{W_k,\theta_k}$. By (a),
		\begin{align}
			\norm{G_k - G}_{L^1} \le \norm{\mathcal{T}_k(G_k) - \mathcal{T}_k(G)}_{L^1} + \norm{\mathcal{T}_k(G) - \mathcal{T}(G)}_{L^1} \le \frac14\norm{G_k - G}_{L^1} + \norm{\mathcal{T}_k(G) - \mathcal{T}(G)}_{L^1},
		\end{align}
		so it suffices to show that $\norm{\mathcal{T}_k(G) - \mathcal{T}(G)}_{L^1} \to 0$. Let $a_k, u_k$ and $a, u$ be defined as in (a), with $(W_k,\theta_k)$ and $(W,\theta)$, respectively, and with the same $G$. Then $|\mathcal{T}_k(G) - \mathcal{T}(G)| \le |u_k - u|/(\operatorname{Im}z)^2$ and
		\begin{align}
			|a_k - a|(y) \le \frac{C|\theta_k - \theta|}{\operatorname{Im}z} + \frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}z}\int_0^1|W_k - W|(x',y)\,dx'.
		\end{align}
		Using $|1 + a_k|, |1+a| \ge \frac12$, we obtain
		\begin{align}
			|u_k - u|(x) \le 2C|\theta_k - \theta| + 2\int_0^1|W_k - W|(x,y)\,dy + 4C\int_0^1|a_k - a|(y)\,dy.
		\end{align}
		Integrating in $x$ shows that $\norm{u_k - u}_{L^1} \to 0$, which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lem: bridge_density_stability}
		\leavevmode
		\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
			\item For every $k$, $c_W \le \varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} \le C_W$, and $c_W \le W_\infty \le C_W$.
			\item $\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} \to W_\infty$ in $L^1(u)$.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-47: body_md -->
(a) By Lemmas~\ref{lem: typical_table_bridge} and \ref{lem: density_bounds}, $\frac{d\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}}{d(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k})} \in [e^{-2K_1},e^{2K_1}]$, and the claim follows from $\delta \le \rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}\le\delta^{-1}$. The same argument applies to $\pi_\infty$, since $\operatorname{osc}(\kappa_\infty) \le K_1$.
		
		(b) As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: conditioned_model_weak_as_limit}, $\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}$ is the Schr\"odinger bridge between $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$ with reference density $R_k := e^{-\kappa_k}\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k}$, and similarly $\pi_\infty$ is the Schr\"odinger bridge between $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}$ and $\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$ with reference $R_\infty := e^{-\kappa_\infty}\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}$, since $\int \kappa_\infty\,d\pi + D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty}) = D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi\Vert R_\infty)$ for $\pi \in \Pi(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty},\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$. Since $\kappa_k \to \kappa_\infty$ uniformly and the margin densities are bounded and converge in $L^1$, $R_k \to R_\infty$ in $L^1(u)$; in particular, $R_\infty$ is a probability density and $d_H(R_k,R_\infty)^2 \le \norm{R_k - R_\infty}_{L^1(u)} \to 0$. Moreover, $R_k/(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_k}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_k})$ and $R_\infty/(\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\r}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}}{\mathbf{r}}_\infty}\otimes\rho_{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\TextOrMath{\c}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{c}}_\infty})$ take values in $[M^{-1},M]$ with $M = \exp(\sup_k\norm{\kappa_k}_\infty)$ as before Theorem~\ref{thm: conditioned_model_weak_as_limit}, and the margin densities are bounded below by $\delta$. Hence $H(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k},\pi_\infty) \to 0$ by Proposition~\ref{prop: hellinger_stability}, and $\norm{\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} - W_\infty}_{L^1(u)} \le 2H(\pi_{\mathbf{Z}_k},\pi_\infty)$ by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.
<!-- end proof-47 -->
<!-- proof-48: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem: bridge_density_stability}(a), the values $w_{ij}$ of $\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}$ in \eqref{eq: variance_profile} satisfy $c_W \le w_{ij} \le C_W$, so that $C_{\mathbf{Z}_k} \le C_W$; moreover $\norm{W_\infty}_\infty \le C_W$. Let $\theta_k := m_k/(m_k+n_k) \to \theta$ and write $\nu_k := \nu_{\mathbf{S}_k}$.
		
		\emph{Step 1: concentration.} Fix $f$ as in Proposition~\ref{prop: spectral_concentration} and $t > 0$, and let $\tau_k := m\log(eN/m) + n\log(eN/n) = m\log(e\bar s n) + n\log(e\bar s m)$, where we suppress the index $k$. Since $\bar s \ge c_0$ and $m/n \to \gamma$, we have $\tau_k = O\big((m+n)(1 + |\log\bar s| + \log(m+n))\big)$. Let $a := \sqrt{(m+n)\bar s}$ and $\Lambda_f := 4L_1^2 + L_2^2/c_0$. Since $C_{\mathbf{Z}_k} \le C_W$ and $4L_1^2 + L_2^2/\bar s \le \Lambda_f$, we have $\psi(t) \ge \frac{mta}{4L_1}\log(1 + x)$ with $x := \frac{2mL_1t}{C_Wa\Lambda_f}$. Since $\log(1+x) \ge \frac12\min\{x,1\}$ and $a \ge \sqrt{m\bar s}$, we get $\psi(t) \ge \min\big\{\frac{mt\sqrt{m\bar s}}{8L_1}, \frac{m^2t^2}{4C_W\Lambda_f}\big\}$. Both terms are of larger order than $\tau_k$: the first because $\sqrt{m\bar s} \gg 1 + |\log\bar s| + \log m$ (as $\bar s \ge c_0$), and the second because $\log\bar s = o(m)$. Hence $\tau_k - \psi(t) \to -\infty$, so the right-hand sides in Proposition~\ref{prop: spectral_concentration} tend to zero.
		
		\emph{Step 2: the tilted model.} The matrix $\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}$ has independent centered entries with variances $\mathbf{S}_{ij} = w_{ij}/(m+n) \in \big[\frac{c_W}{m+n},\frac{C_W}{m+n}\big]$. We verify the assumptions of the local law for random Gram matrices \cite{alt2017local}, as in the proof of \cite[Theorem~2.17]{duan2026scaling}. The upper bound on $\mathbf{S}_{ij}$ is the flatness assumption (A), and the lower bound implies assumption (B) \cite[Remark~2.8]{alt2017local}. The ratio $m/n$ stays bounded away from $0$ and $\infty$, which is assumption (D). For the moment assumption (C), recall that the $2p$th central moment of $\operatorname{Poisson}(\lambda)$ is a polynomial in $\lambda$ of degree $p$ with nonnegative coefficients and no constant term, so $\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij} - \mathbf{Z}_{ij})^{2p} \le A_p(\mathbf{Z}_{ij} + \mathbf{Z}_{ij}^p)$ for a constant $A_p$ depending only on $p$. Since $\mathbf{Z}_{ij} \le C_W\bar s$ and $\bar s \ge c_0$, this gives $\mathbb{E}\big|(\mathbf{Y}_{ij} - \mathbf{Z}_{ij})/\sqrt{\bar s}\big|^{2p} \le A_p\big(C_Wc_0^{1-p} + C_W^p\big)$ for every $p \ge 1$. Hence, by the local law and rigidity estimates of \cite[Theorems~2.7 and 2.9]{alt2017local}, $d_{\mathrm{BL}}\big(\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}^\top}, \nu_k\big) \to 0$ in probability. Moreover, by \cite[Theorem~2.1]{alt2017local} and scaling, $\nu_k$ is supported in $[0,4C_W]$.
		
		\emph{Step 3: the limit of $\nu_k$.} Let $z \in \mathbb{H}$ with $\operatorname{Im}z \ge 2C_W + 1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: dyson_continuity}(b), applied with $W = \varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k}$ and $C = C_W$, the Stieltjes transform of $\nu_k$ at $z$ equals $\int_0^1 G_{\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k},\theta_k}(x,z)\,dx$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: bridge_density_stability}(b), $\varphi_{\mathbf{Z}_k} \to W_\infty$ in $L^1(u)$, and hence, by Lemma~\ref{lem: dyson_continuity}(c), the Stieltjes transform of $\nu_k$ at $z$ converges to $\int_0^1 G_{W_\infty,\theta}(x,z)\,dx$. Since the measures $\nu_k$ are supported in $[0,4C_W]$, every subsequence has a further subsequence converging weakly to a probability measure $\nu'$ on $[0,4C_W]$, and the Stieltjes transform of $\nu'$ equals $\int_0^1 G_{W_\infty,\theta}(x,z)\,dx$ for all $z$ with $\operatorname{Im}z \ge 2C_W + 1$. Since the Stieltjes transform of a finite measure is analytic on $\mathbb{H}$ and determines the measure, $\nu'$ does not depend on the subsequence. Hence $\nu_k \Rightarrow \nu_\infty$, where $\nu_\infty$ is the unique probability measure whose Stieltjes transform coincides with $\int_0^1 G_{W_\infty,\theta}(x,z)\,dx$ for $\operatorname{Im}z \ge 2C_W + 1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: dyson_continuity}(a) with $C = \norm{W_\infty}_\infty$, $G_{W_\infty,\theta}(\cdot,z)$ is the solution of \eqref{eq: dyson_continuum} described before the theorem for every $z$ with $\operatorname{Im}z \ge 2\norm{W_\infty}_\infty + 1$. It is the limit of the iterates $\mathcal{T}^{j}_{W_\infty,\theta}(0)$, $j \to \infty$, uniformly in $x$ and $z$, and by induction each iterate is analytic in $z$. Hence $z \mapsto \int_0^1 G_{W_\infty,\theta}(x,z)\,dx$ is analytic on $\{\operatorname{Im}z > 2\norm{W_\infty}_\infty + 1\}$, and by the identity theorem and continuity it coincides with the Stieltjes transform of $\nu_\infty$ on $\{\operatorname{Im}z \ge 2\norm{W_\infty}_\infty + 1\}$.
		
		\emph{Step 4: conclusion.} Let $f \in C^2_c([0,\infty))$, so that $f'$ vanishes on $[R^2,\infty)$ for some $R \ge 1$. Then
		\begin{align}
			\Big|\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}}^\top} - \int f\,d\nu_\infty\Big| &\le \Big|\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}}^\top} - \mathbb{E}\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^\top}\Big| + \mathbb{E}\Big|\int f\,d\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^\top} - \int f\,d\nu_k\Big| \\
			&\quad + \Big|\int f\,d\nu_k - \int f\,d\nu_\infty\Big|,
		\end{align}
		where $\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{X}_k$ and $\mathbf{Y} = \mathbf{Y}_k$. The first term tends to zero in probability by Step~1. The second term tends to zero by Step~2 and bounded convergence, since $f$ is bounded and Lipschitz. The third tends to zero by Step~3. The same argument without the first term applies to $\mathbf{Y}$. Since $\nu_\infty$ is a probability measure and $C^2_c([0,\infty))$ is dense in $C_c([0,\infty))$, a standard argument shows that $d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}_k}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{X}_k}^\top},\nu_\infty) \to 0$ and $d_{\mathrm{BL}}(\mu_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{Y}_k}^\top},\nu_\infty) \to 0$ in probability.
<!-- end proof-48 -->
